  URL: https://www.ferc.gov/sites/default/files/2025-12/25_Annual%20Assessment%20of%20Demand%20Response_1212.pdf

- **[S517] Ofgem — Consumer Vulnerability Strategy**  
  Why it matters: shows regulators trying to ensure that vulnerable consumers benefit from, and are not harmed by, increasingly digital, decentralised, and innovative energy services.  
  URL: https://www.ofgem.gov.uk/sites/default/files/2025-04/Final%20CVS%2015042025-20250414111309.pdf

## Payments resilience / offline payments / continuity

- **[S518] Sveriges Riksbank — Recommendations to the general public regarding payments**  
  Why it matters: explicitly turns household payment resilience into preparedness guidance, recommending multiple payment methods, physical cards and PINs, and cash at home.  
  URL: https://www.riksbank.se/en-gb/payments--cash/payment-preparedness/recommendations-to-the-general-public/

- **[S519] Sveriges Riksbank — Offline payments**  
  Why it matters: shows a central bank coordinating offline card payments for essential goods, with up to seven days of disruption coverage and a target of no later than 1 July 2026.  
  URL: https://www.riksbank.se/en-gb/payments--cash/payment-preparedness/offline-payments/

- **[S520] Sveriges Riksbank — Government e-identification as an alternative to BankID for payments is positive**  
  Why it matters: treats concentration in one dominant payment identity method as a resilience and accessibility issue, not just a convenience matter.  
  URL: https://www.riksbank.se/en-gb/payments--cash/payments-in-sweden/payments-report-2026/how-safe-efficient-and-accessible-are-payments/government-e-identification-as-an-alternative-to-bankid-for-payments-is-positive/

- **[S521] Sveriges Riksbank — Difficult to help others with payments**  
  Why it matters: shows that delegated and assisted payment is already a real operational problem as manual methods deteriorate and digital assumptions harden.  
  URL: https://www.riksbank.se/en-gb/payments--cash/payments-in-sweden/payments-report-2026/how-safe-efficient-and-accessible-are-payments/difficult-to-help-others-with-payments-/

- **[S522] European Central Bank — FAQs on the digital euro**  
  Why it matters: sets out the digital euro as a universally accepted public payment method with online and offline use, cash-like privacy for offline payments, accessibility commitments, and proposed mandatory acceptance/distribution.  
  URL: https://www.ecb.europa.eu/euro/digital_euro/faqs/html/ecb.faq_digital_euro.en.html

- **[S523] European Central Bank — The digital euro: ensuring resilience and inclusion in digital payments**  
  Why it matters: explicitly frames the digital euro as business-continuity infrastructure with additional payment rails, distributed processing regions, and fallback options if providers are disrupted.  
  URL: https://www.ecb.europa.eu/press/key/date/2025/html/ecb.sp250904~70ab593276.en.html

- **[S524] European Central Bank — Preparation phase of a digital euro: Closing report**  
  Why it matters: summarizes the project as a public, trusted, resilient, and inclusive payment layer meant to preserve public money in an increasingly digital ecosystem.  
  URL: https://www.ecb.europa.eu/euro/digital_euro/progress/html/ecb.deprp202510.en.html

- **[S525] European Central Bank — Keep calm and carry cash: lessons on the unique role of physical currency across four crises**  
  Why it matters: uses recent crisis evidence, including the April 2025 Iberian blackout, to show that physical cash remains operationally important when digital infrastructures fail.  
  URL: https://www.ecb.europa.eu/press/economic-bulletin/articles/2025/html/ecb.ebart202506_02~1a773e2ca3.en.html

- **[S526] Bank of England — Design note: Offline payments**  
  Why it matters: shows a major central bank explicitly evaluating offline payments for resilience during connectivity disruptions and longer outages.  
  URL: https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/report/2025/offline-payments-design-note

- **[S527] Bank of England — Digital pound update**  
  Why it matters: places resilience, privacy, and consumer protection inside the core design logic for a possible public digital-payment scheme.  
  URL: https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/report/2025/digital-pound-update-october

- **[S528] Bank of England — Operational resilience of the financial sector**  
  Why it matters: states that firms and FMIs must maintain important business services through cyberattacks, outages, supplier failure, severe weather, and other disruptions.  
  URL: https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/financial-stability/operational-resilience-of-the-financial-sector

- **[S529] Federal Reserve Board — A Robust Risk Framework for Offline Payments**  
  Why it matters: says offline digital payments are emerging as a vital part of the payment ecosystem during crises or natural disasters and proposes an end-to-end secure offline model.  
  URL: https://www.federalreserve.gov/econres/feds/a-robust-risk-framework-for-offline-payments.htm

- **[S530] Federal Reserve Board — Using Service Provider Connections to Model Operational Payment Networks**  
  Why it matters: shows that even with workarounds, large provider outages can still imply disruptions on the order of hundreds of billions of dollars, making payment continuity a systemic issue.  
  URL: https://www.federalreserve.gov/econres/notes/feds-notes/using-service-provider-connections-to-model-operational-payment-networks-20250103.html

- **[S531] NIST — Responsible Use of Positioning, Navigation and Timing Services**  
  Why it matters: states that GPS timing underpins cellphone calls, financial timestamps, and transport safety; frames PNT resilience as a critical-infrastructure issue; and notes NIST’s alternate fibre-based time service.  
  URL: https://www.nist.gov/pnt

- **[S532] NIST — An Evaluation of Dependencies of Critical Infrastructure Timing Systems on the Global Positioning System (GPS)**  
  Why it matters: explicitly treats reliable time as essential to critical infrastructure and names the financial, telecommunications, and electric-power sectors as timing-dependent domains.  
  URL: https://www.nist.gov/publications/evaluation-dependencies-critical-infrastructure-timing-systems-global-positioning

- **[S533] GPS.gov — Program Funding**  
  Why it matters: shows current U.S. civil PNT policy moving toward real-time GNSS monitoring, interference detection, complementary PNT adoption, and resilient operations under disruption or manipulation.  
  URL: https://www.gps.gov/program-funding-0

- **[S534] ICAO — Protect satellite navigation from interference, UN agencies urge**  
  Why it matters: shows ICAO, ITU, and IMO jointly elevating jamming and spoofing into a cross-sector issue affecting aviation, maritime, and telecommunications, with explicit calls for resilient timing and maintained fallback infrastructure.  
  URL: https://www.icao.int/news/protect-satellite-navigation-interference-un-agencies-urge

- **[S535] EASA — EASA and IATA outline comprehensive plan to mitigate GNSS interference risks**  
  Why it matters: documents sharply rising interference events and pushes backup navigation aids, monitoring, and contingency planning into mainstream aviation resilience work.  
  URL: https://www.easa.europa.eu/en/newsroom-and-events/press-releases/easa-and-iata-outline-comprehensive-plan-mitigate-gnss

- **[S536] European Commission Joint Research Centre — Complementary Position, Navigation and Timing**  
  Why it matters: frames the terrestrial timing backbone as the first step of a complementary PNT ecosystem and treats independent time distribution as a resilience-enabling infrastructure layer for Europe.  
  URL: https://joint-research-centre.ec.europa.eu/projects-and-activities/complementary-position-navigation-and-timing_en

- **[S537] GOV.UK — Cutting-edge timing technology to protect vital services**  
  Why it matters: shows the UK explicitly funding a national backup timing network to protect phone networks, banking, transport, and other critical services from satellite disruption.  
  URL: https://www.gov.uk/government/news/cutting-edge-timing-technology-to-protect-vital-services

- **[S538] National Physical Laboratory — National Timing Centre (NTC) programme**  
  Why it matters: names time as an essential utility, describes nationally distributed terrestrial timing infrastructure, and shows timing-service nodes already being used for sectors such as finance.  
  URL: https://www.npl.co.uk/ntc

- **[S543] European Commission — The Digital Networks Act - Questions and Answers**  
  Why it matters: makes resilience, preparedness, dependency reduction, and crisis-risk management explicit objectives of EU connectivity policy.

- **[S544] Federal Register — Wireless Emergency Alerts and the Emergency Alert System**

- **[S545] FCC — 988 Suicide & Crisis Lifeline**  
  Why it matters: shows the FCC treating crisis-line access as communications infrastructure, including rules to improve outage reporting and georouting for texts to 988.

- **[S546] FCC — Proposes Improved Accessibility for Next Generation 911 Networks**  
  Why it matters: frames NG911 around reliability, interoperability, and improved support for voice, text, data, and video communications, including better accessibility.

- **[S547] Ofcom — Compliance programme into access to emergency services**

- **[S548] ITU — Emergency Telecommunications - BDT Year in Review 2025**  
  Why it matters: shows public warning dissemination becoming a standing operating layer, with country support for NETPs, cell-broadcast feasibility studies, and mobile early-warning systems.

- **[S549] ITU — National Emergency Telecommunication Plans**  
  Why it matters: defines telecom emergency planning as a cross-government governance framework involving hazards, operator landscapes, service penetration, procedures, and contingency roles beyond the ICT sector.

- **[S550] UNDRR — Global Status of Multi-Hazard Early Warning Systems 2025**  
  Why it matters: says improvements in digital infrastructure, mobile accessibility and affordability, and rollout of cell broadcast and location-based SMS will continue to transform warning dissemination.

## Temperature-controlled continuity / cold chains / refrigeration resilience

- **[S551] FAO — The role of energy in agrifood systems transformation**  
  Why it matters: states that inadequate refrigeration and storage lead to roughly 14% of food loss between harvest and retail, making preservation capacity a structural food-systems variable.  
  URL: https://openknowledge.fao.org/bitstreams/c0aa2581-8f47-4c48-b762-e1c26f9911f7/download

- **[S552] WHO — Supply chain and logistics**  
  Why it matters: says successful immunization programmes depend on end-to-end systems that ensure rigorous temperature control and uninterrupted vaccine availability.  
  URL: https://www.who.int/teams/immunization-vaccines-and-biologicals/essential-programme-on-immunization/supply-chain

- **[S553] WHO — Immunization supply chain guidance and tools**  
  Why it matters: shows cold-chain equipment, temperature mapping, temperature monitoring, and inventory / sizing tools becoming explicit management surfaces, with fresh updates in 2025 and 2026.

- **[S554] WHO — Department of Regulation and Prequalification annual report 2025**  
  Why it matters: lists cold rooms, refrigerated vehicles, refrigerators, freezers, and temperature-monitoring devices as formal prequalification categories, and documents updated vaccine-packaging / shipping guidance plus revised refrigerated-transport specifications.  
  URL: https://cdn.who.int/media/docs/default-source/medicines/regulatory-updates/rpq/who_rpq_annualreport2025.pdf?download=true&sfvrsn=afe3907_3

- **[S555] UNICEF Supply Division — Solar-powered refrigeration systems price data**  
  Why it matters: shows solar refrigeration becoming a standardised, transparent procurement class for vaccine storage in places with weak or intermittent electricity.

- **[S556] UNICEF Burundi — UNICEF installs solar refrigerators to strengthen Burundi’s immunization cold chain**  
  Why it matters: gives a concrete operational case in which solar refrigeration stabilises vaccine temperatures and availability under frequent power cuts and limited electricity access.

- **[S557] UNEP — Global Cooling Watch 2025**  
  Why it matters: says cooling now covers cold chains protecting food, medicines, and crops and explicitly frames cooling as essential infrastructure.  
  URL: https://www.unep.org/resources/global-cooling-watch-2025

- **[S558] UNEP — Sustainable Cold Chains**  
  Why it matters: shows resilient, efficient, climate-friendly cold chains being built as a multi-country development programme with training, demonstration sites, and scalable business models.

- **[S559] IEA — Electricity security matters more than ever**  
  Why it matters: explicitly identifies food-safety risk to the cold chain as a major indirect impact of blackouts, linking preservation capacity to power-system governance.  
  URL: https://www.iea.org/reports/power-systems-in-transition/electricity-security-matters-more-than-ever

- **[S560] IEA — Recommendation 4: Community cooling hubs in India**  
  Why it matters: shows food cold chains being integrated with community health facilities, schools, social facilities, and emergency services, pointing toward multi-use local cooling infrastructure.  
  URL: https://www.iea.org/reports/recommendations-of-the-global-commission-on-people-centred-clean-energy-transitions/recommendation-4

- **[S561] FDA — Initiative to benchmark and profile monoclonal antibody and large biomolecule stability**  
  Why it matters: shows biologic distribution being constrained by cold-storage burdens and temperature uncertainty, extending the preservation problem beyond routine vaccines.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/emergency-preparedness-and-response/innovative-technologies/initiative-benchmark-and-profile-monoclonal-antibody-and-large-biomolecule-stability

  Why it matters: shows authoritative geospatial attribution being fused with address data and building-access points being treated as useful layers for emergency response, accessibility, and planning.

- **[S569] Office for National Statistics — The plan for ONS economic statistics**

- **[S570] India Post — DIGIPIN**  
  Why it matters: explicitly frames standardized geo-coded addressing as digital public infrastructure and “Address as a Service,” with offline use, rural coverage, and privacy-preserving design.

- **[S571] UPU — Union Postale Winter 2025/2026 (digital addressing interoperability)**  
  Why it matters: shows digital addressing becoming an interoperability, validation, privacy, and cross-border data-quality problem rather than only a domestic postal-format problem.

- **[S573] European Commission — Commission launches consultation on the Digital Product Passport**  
  Why it matters: shows the passport moving from legislative concept to governed service layer, with explicit questions about data storage, management by service providers, and possible certification requirements.

  Why it matters: specifies a battery passport accessed through a QR code and unique identifier, requires open and interoperable machine-readable data without vendor lock-in, and links new passports to old ones when batteries are reused, repurposed, or remanufactured.

- **[S575] European Commission — New EU rules on the safety and sustainability of construction products**  
  Why it matters: shows digital product passports spreading into construction, where they bundle conformity, safety, and use information and support building-level carbon accounting.

- **[S577] FDA — Drug Supply Chain Security Act (DSCSA)**

- **[S578] FDA — DSCSA Standards for the Interoperable Exchange of Information for Tracing of Certain Human, Finished, Prescription Drugs**  
  Why it matters: shows FDA specifying secure, interoperable electronic data exchange standards as the connective tissue for object-level pharmaceutical tracing.

- **[S579] FDA — FSMA Final Rule on Requirements for Additional Traceability Records for Certain Foods**

- **[S580] FDA — Global Unique Device Identification Database (GUDID)**

- **[S581] UNECE — Digital Product Passport | UN Transparency Protocol**

## Conformity assessment / certification / proof-of-rule

- **[S592] WTO — Technical barriers to trade**  
  Why it matters: shows that technical regulations, standards, and conformity assessment procedures are governed together at the multilateral trade layer because they shape trust and market access.

- **[S593] WTO — Members add good practice guide to TBT transparency tools, advance work on triennial review**

- **[S594] European Commission — Notified bodies for medical devices**  
  Why it matters: defines notified bodies as designated organisations that assess conformity before certain products can be placed on the market, making assurance capacity an explicit market-access layer.  
  URL: https://health.ec.europa.eu/medical-devices-topics-interest/notified-bodies-medical-devices_en

- **[S595] European Commission — New regulations (medical devices sector)**  
  Why it matters: shows the Commission redesigning medical-device rules to make procedures easier, faster, and more digital, which is a sign that the proving apparatus itself has become strategically important.

- **[S596] European Commission — Navigating the AI Act**

- **[S597] European Commission — Cyber Resilience Act: conformity assessment**  
  Why it matters: shows that some important and critical digital products require notified-body participation or mandatory third-party assessment, turning cybersecurity proof into a structured market-access pathway.  
  URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/cra-conformity-assessment

- **[S598] ENISA — Find a Conformity Assessment Body**  
  Why it matters: shows EU cybersecurity certification now depending on nationally accredited and Commission-notified conformity assessment bodies listed for use under EU schemes.  
  URL: https://certification.enisa.europa.eu/take-action/find-conformity-assessment-body_en

- **[S599] ENISA — EUCC Certification Scheme**  
  Why it matters: shows the EU building a commonly understood assessment pathway for certifying ICT products, with assurance levels and supporting accreditation documents.  
  URL: https://certification.enisa.europa.eu/certification-library/eucc-certification-scheme_en

- **[S600] WHO — What We Do | Prequalification of Medical Products**  
  Why it matters: frames WHO prequalification as a service to ensure key health products meet global standards of quality, safety, and efficacy.  
  URL: https://extranet.who.int/prequal/about/what-we-do

- **[S601] WHO — Procurement agencies | Medicines prequalification**  
  Why it matters: says a product deemed prequalified after WHO approval becomes eligible to participate in UN procurement tenders, showing assessment status functioning as a procurement gate.  
  URL: https://extranet.who.int/prequal/medicines/procurement-agencies

- **[S602] FDA — Accredited Third-Party Certification Program**  
  Why it matters: shows certification being used both to qualify for expedited food import review and, in some cases, to prevent potentially harmful food from entering the United States.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/food/importing-food-products-united-states/accredited-third-party-certification-program

- **[S603] FDA — Import Certification**  
  Why it matters: shows certification and other recognised assurances becoming conditions of admission that preserve market access for compliant imported foods while responding to recurring risk.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/food/importing-food-products-united-states/import-certification


## Dependency mapping / third-party registers / critical-service topology

- **[S604] ESMA — Digital Operational Resilience Act (DORA)**  
  Why it matters: shows the ESAs collecting DORA registers of information from competent authorities, which makes dependency inventories part of live EU financial supervision rather than private internal documentation.

- **[S605] European Banking Authority — Preparations for reporting of DORA registers of information**  
  Why it matters: states that financial entities in scope need comprehensive registers of ICT third-party arrangements and that the registers support risk monitoring, supervision, and the designation of critical ICT providers.

- **[S606] European Banking Authority — The European Supervisory Authorities designate critical ICT third-party providers under DORA**  
  Why it matters: shows that EU authorities are already using data drawn from registers of information to identify and formally designate critical providers for oversight.

- **[S607] European Commission — Toolbox to improve ICT supply chain security**  
  Why it matters: frames ICT supply-chain dependency identification, assessment, and mitigation as a common EU governance problem and explicitly addresses dependencies on high-risk suppliers.  
  URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/library/toolbox-improve-ict-supply-chain-security

- **[S608] CISA, NSA, and global partners — Shared Vision of Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) Guidance**  
  Why it matters: treats the SBOM as a formal record of software components and supply-chain relationships, showing component dependency structure turning into a standardized assurance artefact.

- **[S609] Bank of England — Operational resilience of the financial sector**  
  Why it matters: shows UK financial resilience policy explicitly treating third-party supplier failure as part of the disruption set firms must be able to withstand when delivering important business services.  
  URL: https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/financial-stability/operational-resilience-of-the-financial-sector

- **[S610] Bank of England — Updated outsourcing and third-party risk management: central counterparties**  
  Why it matters: defines mapping as documenting the resources needed for important business services and explicitly extends that mapping to third parties, supply chains, and sub-outsourcing arrangements.  
  URL: https://www.bankofengland.co.uk/paper/2026/ss/updated-outsourcing-and-third-party-risk-management-ss-central-counterparties

- **[S611] APRA — CPS 230 Operational Risk Management**  
  Why it matters: requires regulated entities to maintain and annually submit a register of material service providers, manage fourth-party risk, and assess concentration and location risk in provider arrangements.  
  URL: https://handbook.apra.gov.au/standard/cps-230

- **[S612] APRA — Operational risk management**  
  Why it matters: turns the prudential requirement into an operational submission artefact through a published material-service-provider register template and deadline.  
  URL: https://www.apra.gov.au/operational-risk-management

- **[S613] Department of Energy — State Energy Security Plan (SESP) Resources**  
  Why it matters: shows cross-sector interdependency diagrams and energy supply-chain diagrams being offered as standard planning tools, extending dependency mapping from firm oversight into state-level infrastructure governance.  
  URL: https://www.energy.gov/ceser/state-energy-security-plan-sesp-resources


## Classification systems / code lists / enforceable categories

- **[S614] World Customs Organization — What is the Harmonized System (HS)?**

- **[S622] GLEIF — The verifiable LEI (vLEI)**

- **[S623] SAM.gov — Entity Registration and the Unique Entity ID**

- **[S626] European Commission — Import Control System 2 (ICS2)**

- **[S627] European Commission — New Computerised Transit System (NCTS)**

- **[S628] European Commission — Proposal for a Regulation on the establishment of European Business Wallets**  
  Why it matters: shows an explicit move toward a harmonised framework in which economic operators and public bodies identify, authenticate, exchange data, and manage role authority through wallet-based infrastructure.

- **[S629] EUR-Lex — Commission Implementing Regulation (EU) 2024/2956 (DORA registers of information)**

- **[S644] ISO 20022 — About ISO 20022**

- **[S648] European Commission — eForms**

  URL: https://travel.state.gov/content/travel/en/legal/visa-law0/visa-bulletin/2026/visa-bulletin-for-april-2026.html

- **[S682] U.S. Department of State — Immigrant Visa Scheduling Status Tool**  
  Why it matters: shows visa access depending on a second queue layer at the consular post level, where interviews are scheduled by documentarily-complete date only when a visa is available.  
  URL: https://travel.state.gov/content/travel/en/us-visas/visa-information-resources/iv-wait-times.html


## Exclusion lists / deny-lists / negative clearance

- **[S683] European Commission — Early Detection and Exclusion System (EDES)**  
  Why it matters: shows EU funding access already governed through a formal system that can exclude unreliable counterparties, impose penalties, and publish severe cases to protect the Union’s financial interests.  
  URL: https://commission.europa.eu/strategy-and-policy/eu-budget/how-it-works/annual-lifecycle/implementation/anti-fraud-measures/edes_en

- **[S684] Acquisition.gov — FAR Subpart 9.4: Debarment, Suspension, and Ineligibility**  
  Why it matters: shows U.S. federal exclusions already standardized in SAM and made effective across the executive branch rather than treated as isolated agency decisions.  
  URL: https://www.acquisition.gov/far/subpart-9.4

- **[S685] SAM.gov — Exclusion Types**  
  Why it matters: shows exclusion states now normalized into machine-usable categories such as prohibition/restriction, pending ineligibility, completed ineligibility, and voluntary exclusion.  
  URL: https://sam.gov/entity-information/resources/exclusion-types

- **[S686] HHS OIG — Exclusions / LEIE overview**  
  Why it matters: shows excluded people and entities can receive no federal health-care payment and that routine hiring or contracting against the LEIE is itself a liability surface.  
  URL: https://oig.hhs.gov/exclusions/

- **[S687] HHS OIG — LEIE Downloadable Databases**  
  Why it matters: shows health-care exclusion status already distributed as a complete downloadable database with a structured record layout and regular replacement cadence.  
  URL: https://oig.hhs.gov/exclusions/exclusions_list.asp

- **[S688] BIS — Denied Persons List (DPL)**  
  Why it matters: shows export-control exclusion already maintained as a current list with recent changes and CSV export for integration into operational screening systems.  
  URL: https://www.bis.gov/licensing/end-user-guidance/denied-persons-list-dpl

- **[S689] OFAC — Sanctions List Service**  
  Why it matters: shows sanctions screening increasingly distributed as current downloadable data, custom datasets, and searchable infrastructure rather than static pages alone.  
  URL: https://ofac.treasury.gov/sanctions-list-service

- **[S690] eCFR — 49 CFR Part 1560, Secure Flight Program**  
  Why it matters: shows air travel already depending on a standing watch-list matching program that compares traveler data against federal lists.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-49/subtitle-B/chapter-XII/subchapter-C/part-1560

- **[S691] World Bank — Listing of Ineligible Firms and Individuals**  
  Why it matters: shows debarment and cross-debarment already functioning as a transnational exclusion layer for development-financed contracts, with frequent list updates.  
  URL: https://www.worldbank.org/en/projects-operations/procurement/debarred-firms


## Simulation / digital twins / model-informed decision systems

- **[S692] European Commission — Destination Earth (DestinE) - digital model of the earth**  
  Why it matters: shows the EU explicitly building a highly accurate digital model of the Earth to monitor, simulate, and predict natural and human interactions, linking simulation directly to disaster preparedness, adaptation, and socioeconomic analysis.  
  URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/destination-earth

- **[S693] ECMWF — Third phase of Destination Earth confirmed**  
  Why it matters: shows the Earth-system digital-twin layer moving from development into an operational phase focused on running and evolving the twins and producing AI-ready datasets.  
  URL: https://www.ecmwf.int/en/about/media-centre/news/2026/third-phase-destination-earth-confirmed

- **[S694] European Commission — Smart cities and communities**  
  Why it matters: shows local digital twins being treated as reusable public tooling for traffic, pollution, public-health, emergency-response, and planning scenarios rather than as one-off smart-city demos.  
  URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/smart-cities-and-communities

- **[S695] NOAA NESDIS — Weather monitoring and modeling could improve with digital twin technology**  
  Why it matters: shows NOAA exploring digital twin environments to rapidly build and examine weather scenarios and improve environmental data products and user-facing insight.  
  URL: https://www.nesdis.noaa.gov/news/nesdis-joint-venture-partnerships-study-determines-noaa-weather-monitoring-and-modeling-could-improve-digital-twin-technology

- **[S696] U.S. Department of Energy — Artificial Intelligence Strategy (2025)**  
  Why it matters: shows DOE treating AI digital twins and the GEMINA program as part of real-time simulation, predictive maintenance, and advanced-reactor operations rather than as peripheral research curiosities.  
  URL: https://www.energy.gov/sites/default/files/2025-09/EXEC-2025-010630%20-%20250923_%20DOE%20AI%20Strategy%20VFinal.pdf

- **[S697] GOV.UK — UK Infrastructure: A 10 Year Strategy**  
  Why it matters: says the National Digital Twin Programme is developing standards, frameworks, methodologies, and tools intended to provide the foundation of a functioning market in digital twins.  
  URL: https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/6853c5db99b009dcdcb73649/UK_Infrastructure_A_10_Year_Strategy_Web_Accessible.pdf

- **[S698] GOV.UK — Clean Flexibility Roadmap**  
  Why it matters: shows digital-twin-adjacent planning moving into live energy-system governance through data-sharing infrastructure, strategic modeling, coordination with the National Digital Twin Programme, and more transparent shared assumptions.  
  URL: https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/clean-flexibility-roadmap/clean-flexibility-roadmap

- **[S699] FDA — Division of Pharmacometrics**  
  Why it matters: states that design-by-simulation is now a common practice in new drug development and that FDA has formal support programmes for model-informed development.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/about-fda/cder-offices-and-divisions/division-pharmacometrics

- **[S700] FDA — Model-Informed Drug Development Paired Meeting Program**  
  Why it matters: shows FDA formally integrating computational models into drug development and regulatory review through a standing programme rather than through ad hoc acceptance alone.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/drugs/development-resources/model-informed-drug-development-paired-meeting-program

- **[S701] EMA — ICH M15 guideline on general principles for model-informed drug development**  
  Why it matters: provides a harmonized assessment framework for MIDD evidence and explicitly defines computational modeling and simulation as evidence-generation tools for drug developers and regulators.  
  URL: https://www.ema.europa.eu/en/documents/scientific-guideline/ich-m15-guideline-general-principles-model-informed-drug-development-step-5_en.pdf

- **[S702] NIST — Digital Twin Standardization**  
  Why it matters: makes clear that digital twins will remain fragmented and expensive without common standards, interoperability, and reusable definitions.  
  URL: https://www.nist.gov/digital-twins/digital-twin-standardization

- **[S703] NIST — Security and Trust Considerations for Digital Twin Technology (IR 8356)**  
  Why it matters: treats digital twins as a broad capability whose deployment depends not only on modeling power but on security, trust, interoperable tools, and explicit assurance thinking.  
  URL: https://csrc.nist.gov/news/2025/nist-releases-nist-ir-8356


## Model credibility / validation / admissibility

- **[S704] FDA — Assessing the Credibility of Computational Modeling and Simulation in Medical Device Submissions**  
  Why it matters: provides a final FDA risk-informed framework for credibility assessment of computational modeling and simulation used in device submissions, showing model admissibility becoming an explicit regulatory surface.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents/assessing-credibility-computational-modeling-and-simulation-medical-device-submissions

- **[S705] FDA — Considerations for the Use of Artificial Intelligence To Support Regulatory Decision-Making for Drug and Biological Products**  
  Why it matters: shows FDA extending the same credibility logic to AI, explicitly tying model credibility to a particular context of use in regulatory decision-making.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents/considerations-use-artificial-intelligence-support-regulatory-decision-making-drug-and-biological

- **[S706] NASA — Standard for Models and Simulations (NASA-STD-7009)**  
  Why it matters: shows a major engineering institution formalizing uniform practices, requirements, and acceptance criteria for models and simulations used in consequential work.  
  URL: https://standards.nasa.gov/standard/NASA/NASA-STD-7009

- **[S707] FAA — M&S Issues in the FAA**  
  Why it matters: states that the FAA approves the data rather than the analytical technique and requires applicants to show that analysis results are valid, accurate, applicable, and consistent with the assumptions of the problem.  
  URL: https://www.faa.gov/aircraft/air_cert/design_approvals/dah/m_s_issues

- **[S708] NIST — Validating and Advancement**  
  Why it matters: says rigorous validation is the cornerstone of trust for digital twins and that continuous validation is necessary if they are to remain trustworthy and useful in practice.  
  URL: https://www.nist.gov/digital-twins/validating-and-advancement

- **[S709] NIST — AI Risks and Trustworthiness / AI RMF**  
  Why it matters: treats “valid and reliable” as a necessary condition of AI trustworthiness and ties that judgment to intended use and context, reinforcing that fit-for-use evidence is central.  
  URL: https://airc.nist.gov/airmf-resources/airmf/3-sec-characteristics/

- **[S710] ASME — Verification, Validation and Uncertainty Quantification (VVUQ)**  
  Why it matters: shows model credibility being formalized through a standing standards program centered on verification, validation, and uncertainty quantification.  
  URL: https://www.asme.org/codes-standards/publications-information/verification-validation-uncertainty

- **[S711] ASME — Digital Engineering**  
  Why it matters: shows credibility-focused standards expanding across medical devices, advanced manufacturing, energy systems, AI/ML, pharmaceutical products, and airframe structures rather than staying inside one niche field.  
  URL: https://www.asme.org/codes-standards/about-standards/technology-highlights/digital-engineering

## Shared scenario baselines / common reference futures

- **[S712] NGFS — Long-term scenarios, narratives and key findings**  
  Why it matters: explicitly says NGFS scenarios are designed to provide a **common reference framework** for analysing the impacts of climate change on the economy and financial sector, which is direct evidence that scenario baselines are being treated as shared governance inputs.  
  URL: https://www.ngfs.net/system/files/2025-11/NGFS%20scenarios%20narratives%20and%20key%20findings_0.pdf

- **[S713] NGFS — Guide to climate scenario analysis for central banks and supervisors (2025 version)**  
  Why it matters: shows the field maturing into codified best practices around scenario design, data, and modelling rather than ad hoc one-off exercises.  
  URL: https://www.ngfs.net/en/publications-and-statistics/publications/2025-guide-climate-scenario-analysis-central-banks-and-supervisors

- **[S714] ECB — Integrating climate risk into the 2025 EU-wide stress test**  
  Why it matters: shows an official EU stress-test exercise being built by integrating the NGFS NDCs scenario into the EBA adverse scenario, which is a clear case of shared baseline worlds disciplining supervisory analysis.  
  URL: https://www.ecb.europa.eu/press/financial-stability-publications/macroprudential-bulletin/html/ecb.mpbu202511_04.en.html

- **[S715] EIOPA — Insurance stress test**  
  Why it matters: shows sector-wide supervisory assessment being conducted against three common scenarios developed at the European system level rather than each institution choosing its own private baseline.  
  URL: https://www.eiopa.europa.eu/browse/financial-stability/insurance-stress-test_en

- **[S716] NREL — Standard Scenarios**  
  Why it matters: shows the energy-planning world maintaining a shared scenario suite designed to capture a range of possible power-system futures for reuse across analysis.  
  URL: https://www.nrel.gov/analysis/standard-scenarios.html

- **[S717] NREL — Annual Technology Baseline (ATB)**  
  Why it matters: provides a consistent set of technology cost and performance inputs for energy analysis and explicitly pairs those inputs with Standard Scenarios, demonstrating that assumption baselines themselves are governed infrastructure.  
  URL: https://atb.nrel.gov/

- **[S718] NOAA — Sea Level Rise and Coastal Flooding Impacts**  
  Why it matters: shows U.S. interagency sea-level scenarios being maintained as shared reference futures and fed into the national climate-assessment process, not just local bespoke models.  
  URL: https://coast.noaa.gov/slr/


## Counterfactual access / institutional inequality

- **[S719] European Commission — Apply AI Strategy**  
  Why it matters: shows the EU explicitly building **access points** into the AI ecosystem through Experience Centres for AI, including access to AI Factories, testing facilities, and regulatory sandboxes rather than assuming all actors can build these capacities in-house.  
  URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/apply-ai

- **[S720] European Commission — AI Factories**  
  Why it matters: says AI Factories provide access to computing time and support services, are open to users including public authorities, and prioritize access for startups and SMEs, which is direct evidence that advanced model capacity is being distributed through shared gateways.  
  URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/ai-factories

- **[S721] EuroHPC JU — Our Supercomputers**  
  Why it matters: shows the European supercomputing layer openly linked to AI and digital-twin workloads and, in some cases, pay-per-use access conditions, reinforcing that high-end counterfactual capacity remains a specialized service layer rather than a universal institutional default.  
  URL: https://www.eurohpc-ju.europa.eu/supercomputers/our-supercomputers_en

- **[S722] ECMWF — The Digital Twins | Destination Earth**  
  Why it matters: shows Destination Earth offering interactive access to models, data, and workflows, with sector applications able to run within the digital-twin workflow itself, which makes direct access materially different from receiving only downstream reports.  
  URL: https://destine.ecmwf.int/digital-twins/

- **[S723] European Commission — EU Agenda for Cities (2025)**  
  Why it matters: says large urban centres are often early adopters of digital tools while less connected territories need support, skills, connectivity, and data access, making the territorial unevenness of advanced planning tools explicit.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/regional_policy/sources/communication/2025-cities-agenda/2025-cities-agenda-communication.pdf

- **[S724] OECD — Emerging divides in the transition to artificial intelligence**  
  Why it matters: shows AI uptake accelerating in a multi-speed pattern, with leaders pulling away and stronger adoption in capital regions, large firms, and knowledge-intensive services, which supports the broader claim that advanced computational capabilities are diffusing unevenly.  
  URL: https://www.oecd.org/content/dam/oecd/en/publications/reports/2025/06/emerging-divides-in-the-transition-to-artificial-intelligence_eeb5e120/7376c776-en.pdf

## Revalidation burden / lifecycle maintenance

- **[S725] FDA — Considerations for the Use of Artificial Intelligence To Support Regulatory Decision-Making for Drug and Biological Products**  
  Why it matters: contains a dedicated life-cycle-maintenance section saying AI model performance can change over time, should be monitored on an ongoing basis, and may require retraining, retesting, and re-execution of parts of the credibility assessment plan after changes.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/media/184830/download

- **[S726] FDA — Marketing Submission Recommendations for a Predetermined Change Control Plan for Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Device Software Functions**  
  Why it matters: shows FDA formalizing iterative model modification as a governed process requiring planned changes, a methodology to develop, validate, and implement those changes, and an assessment of their impact.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents/marketing-submission-recommendations-predetermined-change-control-plan-artificial-intelligence

- **[S727] NIST — Validating and Advancement**  
  Why it matters: says continuous validation is necessary for digital twins to remain high-fidelity, synchronized, and trustworthy enough for optimization, risk management, and resilience, which makes upkeep part of usability.  
  URL: https://www.nist.gov/digital-twins/validating-and-advancement

- **[S728] NASA — Standard for Models and Simulations (NASA-STD-7009B)**  
  Why it matters: embeds models and simulations inside a formal life-cycle structure that includes maintenance, formal assessments, maintained acceptance criteria, and use assessment against permissible uses rather than one-time technical approval.  
  URL: https://standards.nasa.gov/standard/NASA/NASA-STD-7009

- **[S729] OCC — Model Risk Management, Comptroller's Handbook**  
  Why it matters: shows banking supervision treating ongoing monitoring, periodic review, and standards for revalidation after material model changes as core parts of continued model use.  
  URL: https://www.occ.treas.gov/publications-and-resources/publications/comptrollers-handbook/files/model-risk-management/pub-ch-model-risk.pdf
## Baseline translation / climate services / decision-ready intermediaries

- **[S730] WMO — Climate Services Information System (CSIS)**  
  Why it matters: says WMO’s climate-services system guides the development and application of science-based climate information and services in support of decision-making across agriculture, water, energy, disaster risk reduction, and health, which is direct evidence for a formal translation layer between baseline climate knowledge and sectoral action.  
  URL: https://wmo.int/activities/csis

- **[S731] Copernicus — Climate change service**  
  Why it matters: says Copernicus provides climate data and impact information across sectoral areas through the Climate Data Store and that the CDS is designed to let users tailor services to more specific public or commercial needs, showing official support for downstream translation into decision-ready uses.  
  URL: https://www.copernicus.eu/en/copernicus-services/climate-change

- **[S732] Destination Earth — Transforming climate data into sector-relevant information**  
  Why it matters: says the Climate Change Adaptation Digital Twin translates raw climate outputs into impact-sector information and sector-specific indicators while simulations are running, making outputs more manageable and directly useful for climate services and decision-making.  
  URL: https://destine.ecmwf.int/news/transforming-climate-data-into-sector-relevant-information-the-climate-dt-and-one-pass-algorithms/

- **[S733] WMO — Climate Services Dashboard informs climate action**  
  Why it matters: says climate services require engagement between provider and recipient to support specific decision-making processes, reports that only 14% of WMO Members were equipped to provide advanced climate services in 2024, and notes that many still do not provide tailored projections and sector-specific products, showing that translation capacity remains scarce and uneven.  
  URL: https://wmo.int/media/news/climate-services-dashboard-informs-climate-action

## Shared counterfactual utilities / public support layers / access infrastructure

- **[S734] European Commission — Destination Earth (DestinE)**  
  Why it matters: says the digital twins of DestinE will provide users with tailored access to high-quality knowledge for user-specific scenario development in support of evidence-based decision-making, which is direct evidence for a shared public access layer around advanced counterfactual capacity.  
  URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/destination-earth

- **[S735] Destination Earth — Climate Change Adaptation Digital Twin**  
  Why it matters: says prototype climate-digital-twin data are available for users with upgraded access, that the system will support bespoke on-demand “what-if” simulations for selected key stakeholders, and that user needs are built into outputs and simulation design.  
  URL: https://destine.ecmwf.int/climate-change-adaptation-digital-twin-climate-dt/

- **[S736] ECMWF — Third phase of Destination Earth confirmed**  
  Why it matters: shows DestinE entering an operating phase focused on maintaining and evolving the digital twins and digital-twin engine rather than remaining a temporary experiment.  
  URL: https://www.ecmwf.int/en/about/media-centre/news/2026/third-phase-destination-earth-confirmed

- **[S737] EuroHPC JU — Mandate expanded under new regulation amendment**  
  Why it matters: says public-sector and industry users across Europe can benefit from EuroHPC supercomputers via access calls and that AI Factories plus AI Factory Antennas are being implemented as a structured support layer, including free customised support for SMEs and startups.  
  URL: https://www.eurohpc-ju.europa.eu/eurohpc-jus-mandate-expanded-under-new-regulation-amendment-2026-01-20_en

- **[S738] U.S. DOE Office of Science — User Facilities**  
  Why it matters: frames national scientific user facilities as shared public infrastructure that gives researchers access to advanced tools including supercomputers, which is a strong institutional analogue for shared counterfactual utilities.  
  URL: https://science.osti.gov/User-Facilities

- **[S739] U.S. DOE — Supercomputing**  
  Why it matters: says DOE supercomputing facilities are available to researchers from industry and academia and that widespread access is part of how public investment delivers broad benefit rather than remaining closed elite capacity.  
  URL: https://www.energy.gov/topics/supercomputing

- **[S740] U.S. DOE — National Lab Unveils Supercomputer Teton to Users**  
  Why it matters: says Teton can be accessed through the Nuclear Science User Facilities and that NSUF provides nuclear-energy researchers access at no cost to a wide variety of resources, which is unusually explicit evidence for subsidized shared modelling support.  
  URL: https://www.energy.gov/ne/articles/national-lab-unveils-supercomputer-teton-users

- **[S741] U.S. NSF — Infrastructure Cluster (AGS-IC)**  
  Why it matters: says NSF supports NCAR in providing the university community with models, supercomputing, and data resources beyond the reach of any individual university, showing a mature public support layer for otherwise concentrated counterfactual capabilities.  
  URL: https://www.nsf.gov/funding/opportunities/ags-ic-infrastructure-cluster

## Decision-ready indicator bundles / quasi-standards / soft evidence formats

- **[S742] NGFS — Scenarios Portal**  
  Why it matters: says NGFS scenarios provide a common and up-to-date reference point and a global, harmonised set of transition, physical-risk, and macro-financial outputs, which is the upstream condition for recurring downstream bundle formats.  
  URL: https://www.ngfs.net/ngfs-scenarios-portal/

- **[S743] Climate Analytics & IIASA — Climate Impact Explorer Methodology (2025)**  
  Why it matters: documents a globally comprehensive and consistent physical-risk dataset with structured indicator categories for regional and sector-specific analysis, showing translated indicator packs becoming reusable decision objects rather than ad hoc one-off outputs.  
  URL: https://climate-impact-explorer.climateanalytics.org/climate-impact-explorer_methodology.pdf

- **[S744] ECMWF — Ten years of Copernicus at ECMWF**  
  Why it matters: says Copernicus services offer tailored applications and sector-specific indicators that support decision-making across adaptation, risk assessment, and environmental monitoring, which is direct evidence for official indicator-bundle production.  
  URL: https://www.ecmwf.int/sites/default/files/elibrary/81652-ten-years-of-copernicus-at-ecmwf.pdf

- **[S745] WMO — Submission on indicators related to the Global Goal on Adaptation (COP30, 2025)**  
  Why it matters: shows WMO building measurable dashboard, checklist, benchmark-heading, and audit structures around climate-service capacity and integration into plans, which is unusually explicit evidence that decision-ready packages are being standardized enough to compare and assess countries.  
  URL: https://wmo.int/sites/default/files/2025-10/WMO%20COP30%20submission%20Views%20on%20Matters%20Relating%20to%20the%20GGA.pdf

- **[S746] NREL — Annual Technology Baseline (ATB)**  
  Why it matters: says ATB provides a consistent set of technology cost and performance data and explicitly pairs those inputs with Standard Scenarios, showing recurring energy-analysis inputs functioning as shared evidence infrastructure.  
  URL: https://atb.nrel.gov/

- **[S747] NREL — Standard Scenarios / 2024 release**  
  Why it matters: shows annual, reusable scenario bundles built from common assumptions and relied on by a wide range of stakeholders, which is strong evidence that translated scenario packages can become quasi-standard planning objects.  
  URL: https://www.nrel.gov/news/program/2025/nrels-2024-standard-scenarios-now-available.html

- **[S748] EMA — ICH M15 guideline on general principles for model-informed drug development**  
  Why it matters: defines a harmonised framework for evaluating model-informed evidence and gives recommendations for planning, reporting, and submission, showing an adjacent domain converging on accepted evidence shapes rather than free-form modelling outputs.  
  URL: https://www.ema.europa.eu/en/ich-m15-guideline-general-principles-model-informed-drug-development-step-2b-scientific-guideline

## Benchmark governance / reference artifacts / test suites

- **[S749] NIST — Towards Best Practices for Automated Benchmark Evaluations**  
  Why it matters: says consistent practices for validity, transparency, and reproducibility of AI evaluations are only beginning to emerge; presents NIST AI 800-2 as preliminary best practices for automated benchmark evaluations; and explicitly links robust evaluation practice to procurement and implementation decisions.  
  URL: https://www.nist.gov/news-events/news/2026/01/towards-best-practices-automated-benchmark-evaluations

- **[S750] NIST — New Report: Expanding the AI Evaluation Toolbox with Statistical Models**  
  Why it matters: says benchmark-style evaluations are important but often rely on implicit assumptions, conflate different notions of performance, or quantify uncertainty poorly, and that AI 800-3 formalizes evaluation assumptions and measurement targets.  
  URL: https://www.nist.gov/news-events/news/2026/02/new-report-expanding-ai-evaluation-toolbox-statistical-models

- **[S751] W3C — Accessibility Conformance Testing (ACT) Rules Format 1.1**  
  Why it matters: says ACT Rules are designed to enable consistent interpretation of conformance testing, promote consistent results, and yield transparent, reproducible accessibility tests, showing test-rule maintenance acting as a practical governance layer.  
  URL: https://www.w3.org/TR/act-rules-format/

- **[S752] W3C — Official CSS Test Suites**  
  Why it matters: shows W3C hosting official CSS test suites, managing contribution rules, and using the suites to drive interoperability, which is direct evidence that maintained proving grounds can shape practical conformance.  
  URL: https://www.w3.org/Style/CSS/Test/

- **[S753] ASME — Verification, Validation and Uncertainty Quantification (VVUQ)**  
  Why it matters: shows ASME tying simulation credibility not only to standards but also to organized challenge problems and ongoing community maintenance, which supports the broader claim that reference artifacts and their stewards can govern practice quietly.  
  URL: https://www.asme.org/codes-standards/publications-information/verification-validation-uncertainty

## Reference implementations / executable conformance / practical interoperability

- **[S754] W3C — Process Document (18 August 2025)**  
  Why it matters: says implementation experience is required to show a specification is clear enough for independent interoperable implementations, and advises groups to plan demonstrations of interoperable implementations early, often by developing tests alongside implementation work.  
  URL: https://www.w3.org/policies/process/

- **[S755] HL7 — FHIR Downloads / Reference Implementations**  
  Why it matters: says FHIR reference implementations are provided to help implementers adopt the specification, and notes that some are maintained by the FHIR project team even though they are not formally part of the standard, which is unusually direct evidence that practical adoption can center on maintained examples.  
  URL: https://fhir.hl7.org/fhir/downloads.html

- **[S756] OpenID Foundation — Certification**  
  Why it matters: says OpenID certification uses Foundation-developed conformance test suites to promote interoperability among implementations, showing how practical conformity is stabilized by maintained executable artifacts rather than prose alone.  
  URL: https://openid.net/certification/

- **[S757] Open Geospatial Consortium — Technical Committee Policies and Procedures**  
  Why it matters: says the Compliance Program operates as part of standards development to provide resources for testing implementations against OGC standards, which supports the broader claim that interoperability is governed through maintained testing machinery as well as written standards.  
  URL: https://docs.ogc.org/pol/05-020r29/05-020r29.html

- **[S758] European Commission — eForms**  
  Why it matters: says the eForms SDK is the collection of resources, models, and schemas providing the foundation for building eForms applications, which is unusually explicit evidence that an official implementation toolkit can become the practical substrate of a regulatory data standard.  
  URL: https://single-market-economy.ec.europa.eu/single-market/public-procurement/digital-procurement/eforms_en

- **[S759] European Commission — eInvoicing Conformance Testing**  
  Why it matters: says providers can test connectivity against supported reference implementations and that public entities can install their reference implementations and test suites in the shared testbed, showing interoperability practice being organized around maintained executable artifacts.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/DIGITAL/pages/467108669/eInvoicing+Conformance+Testing

- **[S760] European Commission — eSignature: Get started**  
  Why it matters: says implementers can adopt the DSS library as such or use it as a reference implementation, and that solutions based on DSS are already compliant enough to skip the next conformity step, which is unusually direct evidence that a maintained reference implementation can function as a practical compliance governor.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/DIGITAL/pages/467109074/eSignature+-+Get+started

- **[S761] European Commission — EU Digital Identity Wallet Home**  
  Why it matters: says the EU Digital Identity Toolbox will provide common specifications and guidelines together with a reference implementation for the prototype wallet, showing the executable artifact being treated as part of the common interoperability package.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/EUDIGITALIDENTITYWALLET/pages/694487738/EU+Digital+Identity+Wallet+Home

- **[S762] European Commission — The European Digital Identity Regulation was created in a more agile way**  
  Why it matters: says the Commission procured demo wallets and libraries, built a fully functioning reference implementation based on the Architecture and Reference Framework, and used large-scale pilots to test both the specifications and the reference implementation with feedback flowing into standards and implementing acts.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/EUDIGITALIDENTITYWALLET/pages/915931909/The+European+Digital+Identity+Regulation+was+created+in+a+more+agile+way


## Validator services / hosted conformance / machine-verdict pathways

- **[S763] European Commission — Interoperability Test Bed**  
  Why it matters: says the Test Bed is a DIGIT service for conformance testing that can be reused through a shared online installation operated by the Commission, which is a clear signal that conformance checking is being organized as shared infrastructure rather than only local tooling.  
  URL: https://interoperable-europe.ec.europa.eu/collection/interoperability-test-bed-repository/solution/interoperability-test-bed

- **[S764] European Commission — Interoperability Test Bed: Detailed information**  
  Why it matters: says the Test Bed’s validators can create standalone validation services with UI, REST, SOAP, and CLI access, and can also be reused as building blocks inside broader conformance tests, which supports the thesis that validator services become reusable gatekeeping components.  
  URL: https://interoperable-europe.ec.europa.eu/collection/interoperability-test-bed-repository/solution/interoperability-test-bed/detailed-information

- **[S765] European Commission — Interoperability Test Bed FAQ**  
  Why it matters: says validators are reusable software components that can be turned into validation web apps and APIs, used in automated processes, and consumed as DIGIT-managed services, which is direct evidence that validation is being externalized into service form.  
  URL: https://interoperable-europe.ec.europa.eu/collection/interoperability-test-bed-repository/solution/interoperability-test-bed/faq

- **[S766] European Commission — Using conformance testing service**  
  Why it matters: says the eDelivery conformance testing service is versioned and tied to a suite of test cases that validate whether AS4 solutions meet specified security, interoperability, and reliability requirements, which shows hosted checking functioning as a structured admissibility pathway.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/DIGITAL/pages/883824449/Using+conformance+testing+service

- **[S767] Interoperable Europe / SEMIC — New unified SEMIC validator: community usage insights**  
  Why it matters: says SEMIC Validators are online tools used to check whether data and models follow common European semantic specifications and style-guide rules, which is a clean example of public validator services shaping semantic admissibility.  
  URL: https://interoperable-europe.ec.europa.eu/collection/semic-support-centre/news/new-unified-semic-validator-community-usage-insights

- **[S768] Interoperable Europe / SEMIC — Specifications and Validators**  
  Why it matters: lists public validators for DCAT-AP, CPSV-AP, BRegDCAT-AP and others, including REST and SOAP access for integration and conformance testing, showing validator outputs becoming reusable artifacts inside broader workflows.  
  URL: https://interoperable-europe.ec.europa.eu/collection/semic-support-centre/specifications

- **[S769] ASTP/ONC — Conformance Methods**  
  Why it matters: says health IT developers demonstrate conformance using National Coordinator-approved methods and that only approved test methods can be used by authorized testing laboratories and certification bodies, which shows validator and test-method infrastructure directly shaping certification pathways.  
  URL: https://healthit.gov/certification-health-it/certification-process/conformance-methods/

- **[S770] ASTP/ONC — ONC Conformance Test Tools**  
  Why it matters: lists ONC-approved conformance resources such as SITE and Inferno that support health IT certification, which is direct evidence that shared tools increasingly mediate what it means to arrive at formal certification ready for review.  
  URL: https://healthit.gov/certification-health-it/certification-process/onc-conformance-test-tools/

- **[S771] CMS — Compliance and Enforcement FAQ**  
  Why it matters: says impacted payers should use implementation guides and testing tools for FHIR APIs, distinguishes API validation from rule conformance/certification, and requires routine testing and monitoring, which supports the idea that validator and certification layers are coupling more tightly.  
  URL: https://www.cms.gov/priorities/burden-reduction/overview/interoperability/frequently-asked-questions/compliance-and-enforcement

- **[S772] OGC — Compliance Program: How Certification Works**  
  Why it matters: says implementers use community-developed test suites and the OGC Validator to evaluate alignment with standards before registration or certification, which is a clear example of validator services acting as a practical first gate to recognized compliance.  
  URL: https://www.ogc.org/how-our-compliance-program-works/

- **[S773] OpenID Foundation — Certification**  
  Why it matters: says OpenID certification uses Foundation-developed conformance test suites to promote interoperability, showing formal recognition already depending on maintained test services.  
  URL: https://openid.net/certification/

- **[S774] OpenID Foundation — Leading organisations join OIDF independent conformance test program**  
  Why it matters: says the Foundation is launching an internationally recognized independent conformance test program in Q2 2026 to set a global standard for quality and interoperability, which is unusually direct evidence that validator infrastructure is being elevated toward recognized certifying status.  
  URL: https://openid.net/leading-organisations-join-oidf-independent-conformance-test-program/

- **[S775] W3C — Validation Services**  
  Why it matters: says W3C provides free validation services that help check the conformance of websites against open standards, which shows service-based validation functioning as ordinary infrastructural support for standards compliance.  
  URL: https://validator.w3.org/services

- **[S776] W3C — About the Markup Validation Service**  
  Why it matters: says the Markup Validator checks whether web documents satisfy the constraints defined by technical specifications, which is a canonical early example of conformance being externalized into a callable service.  
  URL: https://validator.w3.org/about.html

## Portable validation reports / mutual recognition / traveling verdict objects

- **[S777] W3C — Evaluation and Report Language (EARL) Overview**  
  Why it matters: says EARL is a machine-readable format for expressing test results and that its purpose is to facilitate processing of results from evaluation tools, validators, and other checkers in a vendor-neutral, platform-independent way, which is direct evidence that conformance outputs are being designed to travel.  
  URL: https://www.w3.org/WAI/standards-guidelines/earl/

- **[S778] W3C — Website Accessibility Conformance Evaluation Methodology (WCAG-EM) 1.0**  
  Why it matters: recommends providing machine-readable reports and specifically points to EARL for that purpose, showing an official standards workflow expecting evaluation outputs to be reusable by other tools and institutions.  
  URL: https://www.w3.org/TR/WCAG-EM/

- **[S779] Section508.gov — Accessibility Conformance Report (ACR)**  
  Why it matters: says ACRs help federal contracting officials and government buyers assess ICT products during market research and proposal evaluation, which is direct evidence that conformance-report objects travel into procurement decisions.  
  URL: https://www.section508.gov/sell/acr/

- **[S780] Section508.gov — Sell Accessible Products and Services**  
  Why it matters: says vendors can use OpenACR to create portable, machine-readable Accessibility Conformance Reports, which is unusually explicit evidence that procurement-facing conformance statements are being redesigned for portability and reuse.  
  URL: https://www.section508.gov/sell/

- **[S781] Section508.gov — Accessibility Conformance Report (ACR) Library**  
  Why it matters: says the ACR Editor generates OpenACR YAML documents designed to help accessibility experts create and share machine-readable reports, showing a public-sector ecosystem beginning to normalize reusable report schemas and libraries of conformance outputs.  
  URL: https://www.section508.gov/accessibility-conformance-reports/

- **[S782] European Commission — Interoperability Test Bed software 1.18.0**  
  Why it matters: says the Test Bed supports XML reports of complete test sessions and individual test steps for machine processing, plus REST retrieval of complete test-session reports, which is direct evidence that validator outputs are being shaped as reusable machine-readable artifacts.  
  URL: https://interoperable-europe.ec.europa.eu/collection/interoperability-test-bed-repository/solution/interoperability-test-bed/release/1180

- **[S783] European Commission — Interoperability Test Bed software 1.24.0**  
  Why it matters: says the Test Bed now supports report signatures and custom XML report metadata, which is strong evidence that report provenance, verification, and transport are becoming first-class concerns rather than incidental output formatting.  
  URL: https://interoperable-europe.ec.europa.eu/collection/interoperability-test-bed-repository/solution/interoperability-test-bed/release/1240

- **[S784] OpenID Foundation — How to submit your certification request**  
  Why it matters: says certification consists in part of completing the tests and publishing the results, which shows conformance outputs being expected to circulate as public or semi-public evidence rather than staying inside the original test session.  
  URL: https://openid.net/how-to-submit-your-certification-request/

- **[S785] OpenID Foundation — Certification Conformance Testing Disclosure and Reporting Policy**  
  Why it matters: says OIDF governs disclosure and reporting of the identity of testing entities and the results they achieve, including their progress through conformance suites, which is unusually direct evidence that test-result portability and publication are now governance questions in their own right.  
  URL: https://openid.net/certification/certification-conformance-testing-disclosure-and-reporting-policy/

- **[S786] OGC — Compliance Program: How Certification Works**  
  Why it matters: says organizations document and test their implementations using community-developed test suites and may register conformant implementations in OGC records, which shows test results becoming durable discoverable records rather than one-off local judgments.  
  URL: https://www.ogc.org/how-our-compliance-program-works/

- **[S787] FDA — FAERS Electronic Submissions: E2B(R3) Standards**  
  Why it matters: says FDA provides an E2B(R3) Validator with real-time validation status and results, which is direct evidence that structured verdict outputs are part of routine regulatory-submission workflows.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/drugs/fdas-adverse-event-reporting-system-faers/fda-adverse-event-reporting-system-faers-electronic-submissions-e2br3-standards

- **[S788] FDA — Specifications for eCTD Validation Criteria**  
  Why it matters: says FDA validation criteria provide error descriptions, explanations, corrective steps, and severity levels tied to whether a submission is considered received, which shows a portable verdict vocabulary carrying real downstream intake consequences.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/media/87056/download

- **[S789] FDA — Specifications for Preparing and Submitting Postmarket Individual Case Safety Reports (ICSRs) for Vaccines**  
  Why it matters: says ACK3 communicates acceptance or rejection status plus validation errors and warning messages for each ICSR, which is a clean example of a machine-readable acknowledgment object traveling across systems with operational consequences for resubmission and receipt.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/media/138670/download

## Validation freshness / expiry / ongoing admissibility

- **[S790] Section508.gov — Integrating Section 508 Data into Asset Management and GRC Tools**  
  Why it matters: says procurement teams should verify conformance status and review test reports before awarding new contracts or renewing existing ICT procurements, require updated accessibility conformance documentation, and reassess IT every 12–36 months or based on risk, which is direct evidence that evidence freshness is becoming a procurement-facing condition.  
  URL: https://www.section508.gov/manage/governance/section-508-into-asset-management-and-grc-tools/

- **[S791] ASTP — Real World Testing**  
  Why it matters: says Real World Testing is an annual requirement, verifies that deployed certified health IT continues to perform as intended, and requires recurring public posting of plans and results on the CHPL, which shows maintained admissibility being organized around explicit refresh cycles rather than one-time certification alone.  
  URL: https://healthit.gov/certification-health-it/real-world-testing/

- **[S792] ASTP — Oversight and Surveillance**  
  Why it matters: says certified health IT modules are subject to surveillance to ensure they continue to meet certification requirements in production environments, which supports the broader point that evidence of conformity increasingly has to stay current after deployment.  
  URL: https://healthit.gov/certification-health-it/oversight-and-surveillance/

- **[S793] OGC — Compliance Testing Program**  
  Why it matters: says products are removed from the Compliant Products Record after three years unless renewed and Compliance Certificates expire three years after issuance unless renewed, which is unusually direct evidence that validation or certification admissibility can have a defined public shelf life.  
  URL: https://portal.ogc.org/files/?artifact_id=7586

- **[S794] OpenID Foundation — Certification Terms and Conditions**  
  Why it matters: says implementers must revoke certifications whose corrected claims no longer remain valid, must promptly update or revoke certifications if material information changes, and must remove or relabel expired, terminated, or revoked certifications, which shows freshness and change-trigger governance entering the certification object itself.  
  URL: https://openid.net/wordpress-content/uploads/2019/03/OpenID-Certification-Terms-and-Conditions.pdf

- **[S795] FDA — Electronic Common Technical Document (eCTD)**  
  Why it matters: says electronic submissions must use a version of eCTD currently supported by FDA and that updates take effect on announced dates, which shows admissibility increasingly depending on supported-version windows rather than only on whether a submission once matched a past standard.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/drugs/electronic-regulatory-submission-and-review/electronic-common-technical-document-ectd

- **[S796] Section508.gov — Accessibility Conformance Report (ACR) Library**  
  Why it matters: presents machine-readable ACR packages together with explicit report dates and latest-download links, which is a concrete sign that report recency is becoming part of how conformance evidence is surfaced, compared, and reused.  
  URL: https://www.section508.gov/accessibility-conformance-reports/

## Public result registries / directory surfaces / market ranking

- **[S797] OpenID Foundation — Certification**  
  Why it matters: says OpenID certification uses Foundation conformance suites and presents public tables of certified implementations by role and profile, which is direct evidence that conformance status is being surfaced as a browsable discovery layer rather than kept private inside testing.  
  URL: https://openid.net/certification-3/

- **[S798] ASTP — ONC Health IT Certification Program Overview**  
  Why it matters: says the CHPL is an authoritative, comprehensive listing updated at least weekly, includes certification status, mandatory disclosures, and compliance activities on product pages, and publishes non-conformity and surveillance information, which shows a public registry becoming an operational status surface for the market.  
  URL: https://www.healthit.gov/resources/onc-health-it-certification-program-overview-2/

- **[S799] OGC — Implementation Database / Certified Compliant**  
  Why it matters: says the database lists only OGC Certified Compliant products and can be searched by standard or provider while also filtering for reference implementations, which is unusually direct evidence that a compliance registry can also function as a comparison and discovery interface.  
  URL: https://portal.ogc.org/public_ogc/compliance/compliant.php

- **[S800] Section508.gov — Recent GSA Efforts to Support Section 508 Compliance**  
  Why it matters: says GSA completed a beta ACR Repository as a centralized location for storing, validating, and sharing accessibility conformance reports across agencies in order to improve reliability of conformance information and strengthen acquisition and risk-management decisions, which shows registry design being treated as procurement infrastructure.  
  URL: https://www.section508.gov/manage/section-508-assessment/2025/gsa-efforts-recent/

- **[S801] Section508.gov — Integrating Section 508 Data into Asset Management and GRC Tools**  
  Why it matters: says inventories and GRC tools should record conformance status, evaluation date, and report links, and that procurement teams should verify conformance status and review reports before award or renewal, which shows status fields and linked conformance artifacts acting as screening data rather than passive records.  
  URL: https://www.section508.gov/manage/governance/section-508-into-asset-management-and-grc-tools/

- **[S802] European Commission — List of qualified trust service providers in the EU**  
  Why it matters: says Member States must publish trusted lists, that the lists are essential for certainty among market operators, and that the Commission provides public signed or sealed versions suitable for automated processing, which shows compliance listings becoming structured market infrastructure.  
  URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/eu-trusted-lists

- **[S803] European Commission — eSignature: Get started**  
  Why it matters: directs users to the Trusted List Browser to choose from over 200 active trust service providers, which is a plain example of a compliance registry also functioning as a practical provider-discovery surface.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/DIGITAL/pages/467109074/eSignature+-+Get+started

## Report-signature trust chains / trust anchors / signature validation

- **[S804] European Commission — eSignature FAQ**  
  Why it matters: says the Trusted List Browser is for browsing rather than validation, that trusted-list signatures are validated on load and then rechecked daily, and that a list may appear unavailable if it cannot be downloaded and validated, which is direct evidence that transportable trust depends on a maintained validation ecology rather than on a static public page alone.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/DIGITAL/pages/880312429/eSignature%2BFAQ

- **[S805] OpenID Foundation — OpenID Federation 1.0**  
  Why it matters: formalizes trust chains ending in trust anchors, says trust-anchor public keys must be distributed securely out of band, defines trust-chain expiration as the minimum expiration among signed statements, and notes that chain validation may fail transiently during topology changes, which is unusually direct evidence that portable trust depends on maintained chain state rather than on one signed artifact alone.  
  URL: https://openid.net/specs/openid-federation-1_0.html

- **[S806] OpenID Foundation — OpenID for Verifiable Presentations 1.0**  
  Why it matters: says verifier metadata may come from an OpenID Federation trust chain, that wallets must refuse requests if trust cannot be established, and that X.509-based request modes require signature and certificate-chain validation, which shows portable presentation exchange depending on trust-path validation and not only on payload format.  
  URL: https://openid.net/specs/openid-4-verifiable-presentations-1_0.html

- **[S807] W3C — Verifiable Credential Data Integrity 1.0**  
  Why it matters: defines standardized mechanisms for authenticity and integrity of constrained digital documents using digital signatures and related proofs, and explicitly frames them as a way to share statements without loss of trust because authorship can be verified by third parties.  
  URL: https://www.w3.org/TR/vc-data-integrity/

- **[S808] W3C — Verifiable Credentials Data Model v2.0**  
  Why it matters: says verifiable credentials can carry validity periods, verification material, and status information, and defines them as tamper-evident claims plus metadata that cryptographically prove who issued them, which supports the claim that portable proof objects increasingly carry their own trust-chain dependencies.  
  URL: https://www.w3.org/TR/vc-data-model-2.0/

- **[S809] European Commission — Digital Signature Service (DSS)**  
  Why it matters: says DSS is an open-source library for creating and verifying interoperable signatures and can be used as a reference implementation, while recent release notes add trust anchors with sunset dates, embedded evidence records, file-cache revocation sources, and signature expiration dates in reports, which is strong evidence that signature-validation environment maintenance is becoming a strategic layer of interoperability.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/DIGITAL/pages/467109107/Digital%2BSignature%2BService%2B-%2BDSS

- **[S810] Interoperable Europe Portal — Interoperability Test Bed software 1.24.0**  
  Why it matters: says PDF reports can support signatures for all report types and XML reports can carry additional project-specific metadata, which is direct evidence that conformance ecosystems are actively turning results into signed portable artifacts intended to travel across systems.
