  URL: https://interoperable-europe.ec.europa.eu/collection/interoperability-test-bed-repository/solution/interoperability-test-bed/release/1240

## Supported-version windows / versioned admissibility

- **[S811] FDA — Electronic Common Technical Document (eCTD)**  
  Why it matters: says electronic submissions must use a version of eCTD currently supported by FDA and that updates will include the date on which new versions go into effect, which is direct evidence that admissibility depends on a moving support window rather than on static technical validity alone.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/drugs/electronic-regulatory-submission-and-review/electronic-common-technical-document-ectd

- **[S812] FDA — eCTD Submission Standards for eCTD v4.0 and Regional M1**  
  Why it matters: gives explicit support-begins dates, requirement-begins dates, and version histories for eCTD v4.0 artifacts and validation criteria, showing that submission admissibility is paced by an official version calendar.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/drugs/electronic-regulatory-submission-and-review/ectd-submission-standards-ectd-v40-and-regional-m1

- **[S813] ASTP/ONC — Standards Version Advancement Process (SVAP) Approved Standards for 2025**  
  Why it matters: lists older standards whose adoption expires on January 1, 2026 and newer versions required by December 31, 2025, which is unusually clear evidence that version windows can determine ecosystem membership even without a fresh substantive failure.  
  URL: https://healthit.gov/resources/standards-version-advancement-process-approved-standards-for-2025/

- **[S814] European Commission — DSS Documentation**  
  Why it matters: says DSS 6.0 switches to `jakarta.*` namespaces and directs `javax.*` users to remain on 5.13, which shows supported-version policy operating as a public compatibility boundary in a signature-validation stack.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/DSS/webapp-demo/doc/dss-documentation.html

- **[S815] European Commission — Domibus**  
  Why it matters: identifies the current release, enumerates supported platforms, strongly recommends upgrading, and warns that WildFly will not be supported after Domibus 5.2, which is direct evidence that platform-support windows can become exclusion surfaces in interoperability infrastructure.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/DIGITAL/pages/467110244/Domibus

- **[S816] European Commission — OOTS Technical Design Documents v1.0.6 (March 2025)**  
  Why it matters: explains that CS 1.0.5 rules were not aware of EDM 1.2.0 entries and that the 1.0.6 patch relaxes those rules, which is a concrete case where rule-maintenance lag can exclude newer artifacts before any substantive defect is shown.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/TDD/pages/900013711/OOTS+Technical+Design+Documents+v1.0.6+March+2025

- **[S817] OpenPeppol — eDEC Code Lists Change Log**  
  Why it matters: records document-type identifiers moving into the official state “Removed,” showing that version-window governance can operate at the code-list level and not only at the whole-product or whole-standard level.  
  URL: https://docs.peppol.eu/edelivery/codelists/changelog.html


## Compatibility shims / bridges / wrapper formats

- **[S818] HL7 — C-CDA on FHIR v2.0.0-ballot**  
  Why it matters: says industry and government both want interoperability between C-CDA and FHIR, and that the guide provides document-level profiles plus initial C-CDA ↔ FHIR mappings, which is direct evidence that maintained bridge artifacts are becoming part of practical interoperability.  
  URL: https://build.fhir.org/ig/HL7/ccda-on-fhir/

- **[S819] HL7 — FHIR v5 Comparison with HL7 V2**  
  Why it matters: says V2→FHIR conversion must decide identity resolution, resource merging, contained-versus-identified resource treatment, and snapshot generation, which shows that migration bridges are interpretive intermediaries rather than mere syntax wrappers.  
  URL: https://fhir.hl7.org/fhir/comparison-v2.html

- **[S820] HL7 — FHIR R4/R5 Conversion Maps for ConceptMap**  
  Why it matters: publishes maintained cross-version conversion maps with explicit transformation logic and conversion-status notes, which is strong evidence that mature standards ecosystems increasingly treat conversion artifacts as part of working interoperability.  
  URL: https://fhir.hl7.org/fhir/conceptmap-version-maps.html

- **[S821] European Commission — The Once-Only Technical System connected to EUCARIS**  
  Why it matters: describes a bridge that intercepts evidence requests, retrieves vehicle data, constructs responses, and avoids duplicative development while connecting many existing evidence providers faster, which is unusually direct evidence that bridge layers become strategic migration intermediaries.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/OOTS/pages/647954495/The%2BOnce-Only%2BTechnical%2BSystem%2Bconnected%2Bto%2BEUCARIS

- **[S822] European Commission — eInvoicing in Belgium**  
  Why it matters: says Mercurius acts as a mailroom that relieves bilateral-connection burdens and that Hermes explicitly bridges the gap for recipients not yet natively equipped, allowing one format for everyone during transition.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/pages/viewpage.action?pageId=822640664

- **[S823] European Commission — eInvoicing in Germany**  
  Why it matters: says Germany supports multiple interoperable transmission routes and accepts ZUGFeRD 2.1 as a hybrid PDF+XML format, which is direct evidence that wrapper objects can become durable compromise artifacts in long migrations.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/DIGITAL/pages/467108886/eInvoicing%2Bin%2BGermany

- **[S824] HaDEA — eTranslation and digitalisation on a mission to break down language barriers**  
  Why it matters: says the eTranslation building block exists to make digital service infrastructures multilingual and provide custom solutions to online services, which broadens the shim thesis beyond data schemas into maintained language-compatibility infrastructure.  
  URL: https://hadea.ec.europa.eu/news/etranslation-and-digitalisation-mission-break-down-language-barriers-2022-02-01_en


## Hybrid wrapper formats / durable compromise objects

- **[S825] German Federal e-Invoicing Portal — What is a ZUGFeRD invoice?**  
  Why it matters: states directly that ZUGFeRD is a hybrid PDF/A-3 document with identical XML invoice data embedded inside, which is unusually clean evidence that one artifact is being designed to serve both visual review and electronic processing.  
  URL: https://e-rechnung-bund.de/en/faq/what-is-a-zugferd-invoice-what-does-zugferd-stand-for/

- **[S826] FNFE-MPE / FeRD — Factur-X 1.08 / ZUGFeRD 2.4 common publication**  
  Why it matters: says the current Franco-German standard is a hybrid PDF+A-3 plus XML invoice, explicitly frames it as useful across different company sizes and automation maturity levels, and shows the compromise object being actively maintained into 2026 rather than fading away.  
  URL: https://fnfe-mpe.org/factur-x/factur-x_en/

- **[S827] HL7 — CDA intro in the FHIR specification**  
  Why it matters: says CDA documents must be deterministically human-readable while still allowing additional information primarily for machine processing, which is strong evidence that dual-view document objects are deliberately preserved in mature clinical standards.  
  URL: https://fhir.hl7.org/fhir/cda-intro.html

- **[S828] FDA — Structured Product Labeling Resources**  
  Why it matters: says SPL is an HL7-approved document markup standard adopted by FDA to exchange product and facility information, showing the ongoing regulatory importance of richly structured label objects rather than plain-readable labeling alone.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/industry/fda-data-standards-advisory-board/structured-product-labeling-resources

- **[S829] FDA — Structured Product Labeling (SPL) Implementation Guide with Validation Procedures**  
  Why it matters: says SPL documents contain product information in both structured text and data-element formats and that human-readable labeling content lives in the `<text>` element, which is direct evidence for a durable readable-plus-structured regulatory object.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/media/84201/download

- **[S830] ETSI — EN 319 162-1 Associated Signature Containers (ASiC)**  
  Why it matters: defines a single digital container that can bind signatures or time assertions together with documents, XML structured data, spreadsheets, or multimedia, which broadens the wrapper thesis from single files to durable evidence containers used in business and government interoperability.  
  URL: https://www.etsi.org/deliver/etsi_en/319100_319199/31916201/01.01.01_60/en_31916201v010101p.pdf

- **[S831] European Commission — Digital Signature Service (DSS)**  
  Why it matters: shows that the Commission still actively maintains tooling that supports ASiC alongside other signature formats, which is evidence that containerized hybrid evidence objects remain live infrastructure rather than historical curiosities.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/DIGITAL/pages/467109107/Digital%2BSignature%2BService%2B-%2BDSS

- **[S832] GSA / Section508.gov — Accessibility Conformance Report (ACR) Library**  
  Why it matters: says current ACRs are published as readable HTML, machine-readable YAML, or a ZIP package containing both, which is direct evidence that public procurement is normalizing paired report objects rather than insisting on a single canonical rendering.  
  URL: https://www.section508.gov/accessibility-conformance-reports/


## Readable/structured divergence / paired-artifact liability surfaces

- **[S833] German Federal E-Invoicing Portal — What is a ZUGFeRD invoice?**  
  Why it matters: says the PDF is the visual component and that an XML copy of the *same invoice*, *identical in content*, is embedded inside, which is unusually direct evidence that hybrid invoice objects depend on maintained sameness across readable and structured layers.  
  URL: https://e-rechnung-bund.de/en/faq/what-is-a-zugferd-invoice-what-does-zugferd-stand-for/

- **[S834] HL7 — C-CDA Artifacts Summary (Narrative Text guidance)**  
  Why it matters: says the narrative block contains the complete human-readable attested content, that structured entries are not a replacement for it, and that “DRIV” is the special case indicating clinical equivalence, which is direct evidence that divergence between readable and structured layers is already a governed risk in mature clinical-document standards.  
  URL: https://build.fhir.org/ig/HL7/CDA-ccda-2.2/artifacts.html

- **[S835] HL7 — CDA intro in the FHIR specification**  
  Why it matters: says human readability applies to authenticated content, that additional machine-processing information may exist that is not authenticated and need not be rendered, and that derivation from narrative into structured content must be describable, which reinforces that cross-layer relation is an explicit governance concern rather than an accidental side effect.  
  URL: https://fhir.hl7.org/fhir/cda-intro.html

- **[S836] FDA — Structured Product Labeling Validation Rules for Drug Registration and Listing**  
  Why it matters: says SPL documents include product information in both structured text and data-element formats, places human-readable content in the `<text>` element, and writes validation procedures for both human and system checking, which is direct evidence that paired readable/structured regulatory artifacts require active conformance governance.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/media/84201/download

- **[S837] FDA — Product Identifiers Under the DSCSA**  
  Why it matters: says the product identifier must carry the NDC, serial number, lot number, and expiration date in both human- and machine-readable formats and notes that failure to comply with the statutory requirement is a prohibited act, which shows that divergence or omission across layers can become a legal and enforcement problem rather than just a usability issue.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/media/116304/download

- **[S838] FDA — UDI Basics**  
  Why it matters: says device labelers must provide the UDI in both easily readable plain text and machine-readable AIDC form, broadening the paired-representation thesis from drug supply-chain identifiers into device labeling.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/unique-device-identification-system-udi-system/udi-basics

- **[S839] GSA / Section508.gov — Accessibility Conformance Report (ACR) Library**  
  Why it matters: says ACRs are published as readable HTML, machine-readable YAML, or a ZIP containing both, and describes OpenACR as reusable machine-readable report data, which is direct evidence that procurement-facing conformance artifacts are becoming deliberately paired objects.  
  URL: https://www.section508.gov/accessibility-conformance-reports/

- **[S840] Section508.gov — ACR / VPAT FAQ**  
  Why it matters: says the government may not proceed with ICT purchase consideration without an ACR except in special cases and that updated ACRs may be required when products change, which makes stale or divergent paired reports a practical procurement risk rather than a mere documentation problem.  
  URL: https://www.section508.gov/sell/acr-vpat-faq/


## Authoritative rendering services / evidentiary choke points

- **[S841] FDA — Module 1 Specifications**  
  Why it matters: says submitters provide only code values for certain fields while display names are shown to reviewers in the review tool, and notes that the `us-regional.xsl` stylesheet was updated for validation and display, which is direct evidence that rendering logic participates in regulatory review rather than merely decorating the source XML.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/media/159382/download

- **[S842] FDA — eCTD Submission Standards for eCTD v3.2.2 and Regional M1**  
  Why it matters: records the removal of an older US regional stylesheet and related supportive files from the supported stack while continuing to update the live submission standards, which is strong evidence that review-layer artifacts have their own maintenance lifecycle and support policy.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/drugs/electronic-regulatory-submission-and-review/ectd-submission-standards-ectd-v322-and-regional-m1

- **[S843] HL7 — Composition — FHIR v6.0.0-ballot4**  
  Why it matters: says the human-readable narrative contains the attested content of a section and must be detailed enough to be clinically safe for a human to read on its own, which makes the human-facing rendering path consequential rather than optional.  
  URL: https://build.fhir.org/composition-definitions.html

- **[S844] Interoperable Europe — ITB User Guide release history**  
  Why it matters: says PDF reports can be produced using external services and can support signatures for all report types, while XML reports can carry additional project metadata, which shows render/report-generation services becoming part of portable evidence production.  
  URL: https://www.itb.ec.europa.eu/docs/itb-ou/latest/changeHistory/

- **[S845] GSA / Section508.gov — Accessibility Conformance Report (ACR) Library**  
  Why it matters: says the same report may be viewed as HTML, YAML, or ZIP and that OpenACR YAML can be viewed through ACR Editor, which is direct evidence that designated evidence viewers are becoming part of how procurement-facing conformance objects are interpreted.  
  URL: https://www.section508.gov/accessibility-conformance-reports/

## Trust-anchor sunset dates / rollover windows / hidden service interruptions

- **[S846] European Commission — eSignature FAQ**  
  Why it matters: says the Trusted List Browser is not sufficient for validation, that trusted-list signatures are validated when a list is loaded and then every day at midnight, and that lists can become visually degraded when they cannot be downloaded and validated, which is direct evidence that trust-list liveness already behaves like an operational condition.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/DIGITAL/pages/880312429/eSignature%2BFAQ

- **[S847] European Commission — DSS releases**  
  Why it matters: the release notes add explicit support for trust anchors with sunset date, which is unusually direct evidence that production validation libraries now need first-class logic for anchor retirement and rollover timing.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/sites/spaces/DIGITAL/pages/467109114/DSS%2Breleases

- **[S848] OpenID Foundation — OpenID Federation 1.0**  
  Why it matters: says Trust Anchor public keys are distributed out of band, the whole Trust Chain expires at the minimum `exp` in the chain, participants must refresh chains when they expire, transient validation failures can happen during topology updates, and trust-anchor key rollover requires overlap, which shows that federated trust is governed by refresh tempo and rollover choreography rather than by static trust alone.  
  URL: https://openid.net/specs/openid-federation-1_0.html

- **[S849] Mozilla Support — Update Firefox to prevent add-ons issues from root certificate expiration**  
  Why it matters: says an expired root certificate can disable add-ons, break content signing, disrupt DRM media playback, and interfere with remote-update-dependent features unless users update Firefox, which is a clean real-world example of trust-anchor expiry becoming a service interruption.  
  URL: https://support.mozilla.org/en-US/kb/root-certificate-expiration

- **[S850] Mozilla — Mozilla Root Store Policy**  
  Why it matters: says Mozilla removes websites trust bits when root key material is older than 15 years, uses a distrust schedule, urges operators to seek next-generation roots years in advance, and sets dated transition requirements for existing dual-purpose roots, which shows trust-anchor sunset turning into explicit lifecycle governance.  
  URL: https://www.mozilla.org/en-US/about/governance/policies/security-group/certs/policy/

- **[S851] Google — Chrome Root Program Policy, Version 1.8**  
  Why it matters: defines phase-outs that stop trust for newly issued certificates after a date, requires cross-certified succession and 90-day transition windows, and sets approximate removal dates for older root key material, which is strong evidence that root sunset and rollover now function as scheduled interoperability constraints.  
  URL: https://googlechrome.github.io/chromerootprogram/

- **[S852] Google Security Blog — Sustaining Digital Certificate Security: Upcoming Changes to the Chrome Root Store**  
  Why it matters: announces concrete default-trust removal for specified roots, explains when the block begins, says affected users will see full-page interstitials, and tells operators to migrate to a new CA, which is direct evidence that root-policy sunsets already create user-visible service interruption.  
  URL: https://security.googleblog.com/2025/05/sustaining-digital-certificate-security-chrome-root-store-changes.html

- **[S853] Mozilla Security Blog — Updated GPG key for signing Firefox Releases**  
  Why it matters: says the GPG key used to sign Firefox release manifests was expiring soon and that Mozilla was switching to a new signing subkey, which broadens the sunset thesis from web PKI into software-distribution verification.  
  URL: https://blog.mozilla.org/security/2025/04/01/updated-gpg-key-for-signing-firefox-releases-2/


## Registry-completeness disputes / authoritative record fights

- **[S854] European Commission — Questions & Answers on Trust Services under the European Digital Identity Regulation**  
  Why it matters: says Trusted Lists are essential for certainty among market operators and continually indicate the qualified status of a provider and its services, which is direct evidence that the public record is treated as an ongoing authoritative status surface rather than a historical archive.  
  URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/faqs/questions-answers-trust-services-under-european-digital-identity-regulation

- **[S855] European Commission — Questions & Answers on Trust Services under eIDAS**  
  Why it matters: says national Trusted Lists have a constitutive effect, that a provider or service is qualified only if it appears in the Trusted Lists, and that users benefit from the legal effect only if the service is listed as qualified, which is unusually direct evidence that registry inclusion itself can determine practical legal status.  
  URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/news/questions-answers-trust-services-under-eidas

- **[S856] ASTP / ONC — ONC Health IT Certification Program Overview**  
  Why it matters: says the CHPL is an authoritative, comprehensive listing updated at least weekly, requires non-conformity and surveillance information to be made public, and tells stakeholders to report issues and check the CHPL regularly, which shows an operational registry whose completeness and freshness directly matter to users.  
  URL: https://www.healthit.gov/resources/onc-health-it-certification-program-overview-2/

- **[S857] ASTP / ONC — Certified Health IT Product List (CHPL) Public User Guide**  
  Why it matters: says CHPL searches exclude products without active certificates by default, details multiple certification-status categories, and explains that retired products are not visible unless specifically filtered for, which is very direct evidence that filter defaults and status visibility can change what ordinary users think the authoritative record contains.  
  URL: https://healthit.gov/wp-content/uploads/2024/03/chpl_public_user_guide.pdf

- **[S858] OpenID Foundation — Certification Conformance Testing Disclosure and Reporting Policy**  
  Why it matters: says the Foundation governs the disclosure and reporting of identities and results achieved through its testing services, which shows that who appears in the visible certification record and how results are reported is already an explicitly governed surface.  
  URL: https://openid.net/certification/certification-conformance-testing-disclosure-and-reporting-policy/

- **[S859] OpenID Foundation — Certified and Uncertified OpenID Connect Implementations**  
  Why it matters: the public pages separate implementations that attained certification from those that have not, and the uncertified page says those implementations are not necessarily known to work, which is direct evidence that list placement itself is already doing trust and discoverability work.  
  URL: https://openid.net/developers/certified-openid-connect-implementations/ ; https://openid.net/developers/uncertified-openid-connect-implementations/

- **[S860] OGC — Implementation Database (Certified Compliant products)**  
  Why it matters: says the public database lists only OGC Certified Compliant products and lets users filter down to certified-compliant reference implementations, which shows registry scope and interface defaults already structuring what counts as visible serious compliance.  
  URL: https://portal.ogc.org/public_ogc/compliance/compliant.php

- **[S861] OGC — Implementation Statistics**  
  Why it matters: distinguishes historically compliant certified implementations from a much larger number of self-reported implementations, which makes the line between current, historical, and merely claimed interoperability unusually legible.  
  URL: https://portal.ogc.org/public_ogc/compliance/www_implementation_statistics.php

- **[S862] Section508.gov — Integrating Section 508 Data into Asset Management and GRC Tools**  
  Why it matters: says agency inventories or GRC systems should serve as a source of record for procurement review, should carry fields such as conformance status, evaluation date, report links, and remediation plans, and should be kept accurate through periodic data-quality checks, which is direct evidence that authoritative record completeness is already becoming a governance obligation.  
  URL: https://www.section508.gov/manage/governance/section-508-into-asset-management-and-grc-tools/


## Local trust overrides / custom roots / trust-policy escape hatches

- **[S863] Mozilla Support — Set up Certificate Authorities (CAs) in Firefox**  
  Why it matters: says setting `ImportEnterpriseRoots` to true causes Firefox to trust root certificates and recommends it for adding trust for a private PKI, which is direct evidence that enterprise browser stacks already expose a sanctioned path for local trust override.  
  URL: https://support.mozilla.org/en-US/kb/setting-certificate-authorities-firefox

- **[S864] Android Developers — Network security configuration**  
  Why it matters: says Android apps can define custom trust anchors, debug-only overrides, and app-wide or per-domain trust customization, which is unusually direct evidence that trust policy can be locally rewritten inside the application layer rather than simply inherited from platform defaults.  
  URL: https://developer.android.com/privacy-and-security/security-config

- **[S865] Google — Use Chrome Enterprise Premium to manage root certificate configurations**  
  Why it matters: says admins can add private root certificates to the Chrome Root Store, classify them as Root, Hint, or Distrust, constrain them to domains or IP blocks, and inspect admin-installed custom local certificates on managed devices, which shows local trust policy already functioning as a managed governance surface.  
  URL: https://support.google.com/chrome/a/answer/16073278

- **[S866] Apple Support — Trust manually installed certificate profiles in iOS, iPadOS, and visionOS**  
  Why it matters: says users must manually enable full SSL/TLS trust for manually installed root certificates, while certificates installed through Configurator or MDM are automatically trusted, which is direct evidence that local trust exceptions are already being governed through installation path and device-management state.  
  URL: https://support.apple.com/en-us/102390

- **[S867] Apple Platform Deployment — Intro to certificate management for Apple devices**  
  Why it matters: says manually installed roots on unsupervised devices require explicit user trust, supervised or MDM-installed roots can disable the option to change trust settings, and manually installed roots on recent macOS are not trusted for TLS by default, which shows local trust override rights being differentiated by platform control and supervision status.  
  URL: https://support.apple.com/guide/deployment/intro-to-certificate-management-depb5eff8914/web

- **[S868] Microsoft Learn — Certificates and trust in Windows**  
  Why it matters: says Windows distributes trusted and untrusted root certificates through CTLs with automatic daily updates by default, but administrators can configure trusted CAs, install private CAs, and redirect trust-list updates to internal servers, which is strong evidence that operator-side trust overrides remain part of mainstream platform governance.  
  URL: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/identity/ad-cs/certificate-trust

- **[S869] European Commission — Digital Signature Service (DSS) documentation**  
  Why it matters: says validators must be given pre-configured trust anchors and that these can be added manually through a keystore or set of certificates in the trusted certificate source, which shows local trust override power at the signature-validation layer itself.  
  URL: https://ec.europa.eu/digital-building-blocks/DSS/webapp-demo/doc/dss-documentation.html

- **[S870] OpenID Foundation — OpenID Federation 1.1 draft 04**  
  Why it matters: says participants begin with configured Trust Anchors and their keys, and may choose among multiple valid Trust Chains according to local policy, which is unusually direct evidence that even highly structured federation trust leaves decisive room for deployment-specific override and selection logic.  
  URL: https://openid.net/specs/openid-federation-1_1.html

## Canonical style packs / template bundles / governance dependency

- **[S871] TED Developer Docs — Visualising a notice**  
  Why it matters: says the eForms SDK provides reusable template files and that, once an EFX translator exists, the view templates will be automatically updated through SDK updates, which is direct evidence that many downstream notice views can inherit shared display logic from one maintained template bundle.  
  URL: https://docs.ted.europa.eu/eforms/latest/guide/visualisation.html

- **[S872] TED Developer Docs — Notice View Templates**  
  Why it matters: says the Publications Office created and shared view templates to standardise how notices are visualised independently of media format and to avoid recoding hardcoded visualisations for each eForms release, which is unusually direct evidence that a canonical template pack is being treated as infrastructure rather than decoration.  
  URL: https://docs.ted.europa.eu/eforms/latest/viewer-templates/index.html

- **[S873] TED Developer Docs — eForms FAQ**  
  Why it matters: says the SDK view templates define how notices are displayed by TED Viewer 2022, that the SDK bundles schemas, Schematron rules, documentation, samples, and other elements together, and that multiple SDK versions remain available while supported by legislation or business rules, which shows template bundles living inside a governed release-and-support regime.  
  URL: https://docs.ted.europa.eu/eforms-common/FAQ/index.html

- **[S874] FDA — Guidance for Industry: M2 eCTD Specification**  
  Why it matters: says a submission should include a stylesheet for presentation and navigation and that a standard stylesheet for viewing the eCTD submission is defined and provided by the ICH M2 EWG, which is strong evidence that canonical stylesheets already sit inside a formal regulatory interchange stack.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/media/71513/download

- **[S875] FDA — Example Submissions using the eCTD Backbone Files Specification for Module 1; Validation Tools**  
  Why it matters: the example submissions repeatedly point `xml-stylesheet` to `us-regional.xsl`, while FDA’s validation-tools material lists the US Regional Stylesheet as a named supportive file with version/support metadata, which shows that stylesheet bundles are maintained artifacts in the operational submission environment.  
  URL: https://www.fda.gov/media/83809/download ; https://www.fda.gov/media/92309/download

- **[S876] HL7 — General Guidance - Consolidated CDA (C-CDA) v5.0.0-ballot**  
  Why it matters: says HL7 has created a stylesheet available for the community to use as-is or customize for vendor implementations, which is direct evidence that a shared stylesheet layer is treated as part of practical document interoperability.  
  URL: https://build.fhir.org/ig/HL7/CDA-ccda/generalguidance.html

- **[S877] OpenPeppol — Peppol BIS Billing 3.0**  
  Why it matters: publishes a stylesheet for UBL instances on the same official download surface as Schematron rule sets, code lists, and example files, which shows styling logic being distributed as part of the usable interoperability package.  
  URL: https://docs.peppol.eu/poacc/billing/3.0/

- **[S878] GSA / Section508.gov — Accessibility Conformance Report Library; OpenACR Editor**  
  Why it matters: says the same OpenACR report can be viewed as HTML, YAML, or ZIP, that YAML can be viewed by opening it in ACR Editor, and that the editor is GSA’s published tool for building reports in the maintained OpenACR format, which broadens the style-pack thesis from stylesheets to canonical editor/view packages for machine-readable evidence.  
  URL: https://www.section508.gov/accessibility-conformance-reports/ ; https://acreditor.section508.gov/

## Listing-scope taxonomies / quiet market boundaries

- **[S879] European Commission — List of qualified trust service providers in the EU**  
  Why it matters: says trusted lists must include qualified trust service providers and the trust services provided by them, that qualification exists only if the provider and service appear in a trusted list, and that optional non-qualified services may also be listed if clearly indicated, which is unusually direct evidence that scope design already determines where status attaches.  
  URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/policies/eu-trusted-lists

- **[S880] European Commission — Questions & Answers on Trust Services under eIDAS**  
  Why it matters: says Trusted Lists indicate the status of the service provider and of the service and that national Trusted Lists have constitutive effect, which reinforces that provider-versus-service scoping is legally consequential rather than clerical.  
  URL: https://digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu/en/news/questions-answers-trust-services-under-eidas

- **[S881] ASTP / ONC — Certified Health IT Product List (CHPL) Public User Guide**  
  Why it matters: says the CHPL is a comprehensive and authoritative listing, explains that CHPL identifiers encode product and version, and notes that default search excludes products without active certificates, which shows certified status already being authored and surfaced at a particular product/version scope.  
  URL: https://healthit.gov/wp-content/uploads/2024/03/chpl_public_user_guide.pdf

- **[S882] ASTP / ONC — CHPL Product Versioning FAQ**  
  Why it matters: says differently versioned marketed products may require a new certificate while some listed versions can encompass later subversions, which is strong evidence that version-family roll-up rules already decide how far a status travels.  
  URL: https://healthit.gov/wp-content/uploads/2025/02/chplproductversioningfaq_12012017.pdf

- **[S883] OpenID Foundation — OpenID Certification**  
  Why it matters: says deployments are certified to specific conformance profiles and organizes certified implementations by implementation type and profile family, which shows that certification scope is already profile-specific rather than merely product-wide.  
  URL: https://openid.net/certification/

- **[S884] OpenID Foundation — Certified OpenID Connect Implementations**  
  Why it matters: says implementations attain certification for one or more certification profiles and then lists conformance profiles per implementation, which demonstrates that one visible implementation name can carry several differently scoped status claims.  
  URL: https://openid.net/developers/certified-openid-connect-implementations/

- **[S885] OpenID Foundation — How to submit your certification request**  
  Why it matters: says implementations certifying for multiple profiles must obtain one certification zip file for each profile, which is unusually direct evidence that the operational certification object is the implementation-as-profile, not just the implementation in general.  
  URL: https://openid.net/how-to-submit-your-certification-request/

- **[S886] OGC — Implementation Database**  
  Why it matters: says the database lists only OGC Certified Compliant products, can be searched by standard and product provider, and can be restricted to certified compliant reference implementations, which shows that public comparison already depends on a chosen scope taxonomy.  
  URL: https://portal.ogc.org/public_ogc/compliance/compliant.php

- **[S887] OGC — Product Specification Implementations (Self-reported)**  
  Why it matters: distinguishes certified implementations from a much larger self-reported population and ties counts to specifications and currently associated registered products, which shows that scope and category boundaries already shape who appears to belong in the serious interoperable market.  
  URL: https://portal.ogc.org/public_ogc/compliance/www_implementation_statistics.php

## Scope-crosswalks / comparability brokers

- **[S888] NVD — Product APIs (Match Criteria API)**  
  Why it matters: says CPE Match Criteria are abstract concepts that are correlated to CPE URIs in the Official CPE Dictionary, do not require the same specificity as a CPE Name, and can return the concrete names associated with a match-criteria UUID, which is direct evidence that automated comparability already depends on a maintained mapping layer between abstract applicability objects and concrete product enumerations.  
  URL: https://nvd.nist.gov/developers/products

- **[S889] NVD — CVEs and the NVD Process**  
  Why it matters: says NVD enrichment associates a CPE Applicability Statement with each vulnerability and that automated processes can reference match criteria against the CPE dictionary to identify vulnerable products in an organization’s systems, which is unusually direct evidence that operational security workflows already depend on maintained crosswalk logic rather than only on source records.  
  URL: https://nvd.nist.gov/general/cve-process

- **[S890] NVD — Upcoming change to the Match Criteria API (/cpematch/) resultsPerPage limit**  
  Why it matters: says some CPE Match Criteria can correspond to thousands or tens of thousands of CPE Names, which shows that the comparability layer can become a large-scale operational service whose design choices materially shape downstream exposure judgments.  
  URL: https://nvd.nist.gov/general/news/cpematch-resultsperpage-update

## Applicability ranges / security-market infrastructure

- **[S891] OASIS Open — Common Security Advisory Framework (CSAF) Version 2.1**  
  Why it matters: says CSAF is for actionable, structured, and validated information about vulnerabilities and their relation to products, uses linkage through IDs instead of repeated prose, and includes explicit VEX product-status and product-version-range handling, which shows applicability maintenance becoming a governed data layer rather than informal commentary.  
  URL: https://docs.oasis-open.org/csaf/csaf/v2.1/csaf-v2.1.html

- **[S892] NTIA — Software Bill of Materials**  
  Why it matters: curates VEX as a distinct resource and says it allows a supplier to clarify whether a specific vulnerability actually affects a product, which shows product applicability becoming a named operational object in the public SBOM ecosystem.  
  URL: https://www.ntia.gov/page/software-bill-materials

- **[S893] ITS / NTIA — 2023 5G Challenge Event**  
  Why it matters: says the Best SBOM prize was awarded to the contestants with the highest rated SBOM and VEX, which is direct evidence that machine-readable vulnerability-status artifacts are already being scored in a live public interoperability program rather than merely encouraged in guidance.  
  URL: https://its.ntia.gov/research/5g/2023-5g-challenge-event

- **[S894] NTIA Blog — One Giant Leap for Mobility: Recapping the 2023 5G Challenge**  
  Why it matters: says SBOM and VEX together expedite operator tasks such as security risk assessment, supply chain management, and vulnerability remediation, which shows applicability artifacts becoming operational workflow inputs for infrastructure operators.  
  URL: https://www.ntia.gov/blog/2023/one-giant-leap-mobility-recapping-2023-5g-challenge

## Backport-proof registries / negotiated trust surfaces

- **[S895] Red Hat Customer Portal — Backporting Security Fixes**  
  Why it matters: says Red Hat backports security fixes to older distributed versions, warns that version numbers alone do not tell customers whether they are vulnerable, and points users to advisories that explain how and when issues were fixed independent of raw version numbers, which is unusually direct evidence that fix status already depends on maintained proof rather than naive version comparison.  
  URL: https://access.redhat.com/security/updates/backporting

- **[S896] Ubuntu — Vulnerability management tools in Ubuntu**  
  Why it matters: says Ubuntu LTS releases provide backported security patches to stable software versions, notes that naive scanners can generate false positives if they assume the latest upstream version is the only fixed one, and positions reliable identification of fixed versus vulnerable software as a core vulnerability-management problem.  
  URL: https://ubuntu.com/security/vulnerability-management

- **[S897] Ubuntu Security Documentation — Ubuntu OSV data**  
  Why it matters: says Ubuntu publishes structured OSV data for all supported releases, maps tracker states such as not-affected, vulnerable, fixed, pending, and deferred into machine-readable status objects, and recommends that users and partners consume OSV, OVAL, or VEX instead of the more changeable tracker surfaces directly, which shows backport-proof turning into a maintained data service.  
  URL: https://documentation.ubuntu.com/security/security-updates/osv/

- **[S898] SUSE Documentation — Backports of source code**  
  Why it matters: says SUSE extensively uses backports and that comparing version numbers can be misleading when judging the capabilities and security of SUSE Linux Enterprise packages, which reinforces that downstream fixed status cannot be inferred safely from upstream-looking version strings alone.  
  URL: https://documentation.suse.com/sles/15-SP7/html/SLES-all/cha-update-backport.html

- **[S899] Red Hat Customer Portal — Security Data**  
  Why it matters: says Red Hat publishes CSAF/VEX documents, OSV records, OVAL definitions, repository-to-CPE mappings, and a security-data API so customers and researchers can produce their own metrics, which is strong evidence that machine-readable fix-lineage proof is already being exposed as operational infrastructure.  
  URL: https://access.redhat.com/security/data

- **[S900] SUSE — Security information in CSAF format**  
  Why it matters: says SUSE offers both CSAF advisory data and CSAF VEX data indexed by CVE for machine import into ticket systems and vulnerability-response workflows, which shows supplier-side fix and exploitability proof moving into reusable status feeds rather than remaining trapped in prose notices.  
  URL: https://www.suse.com/support/security/csaf/


## VEX freshness / expiry governance

- **[S901] OASIS Open — Common Security Advisory Framework (CSAF) Version 2.1**  
  Why it matters: requires tracking fields such as `current_release_date`, `initial_release_date`, and `revision_history`, and defines distribution behavior around change discovery, which shows that machine-readable status artifacts are expected to be maintained as living, versioned objects rather than static notices.  
  URL: https://docs.oasis-open.org/csaf/csaf/v2.1/csaf-v2.1.html

- **[S902] OpenVEX Specification (OpenVEX / GitHub)**

## Reusable exception-case objects / portability layer

- **[S934] OpenVEX Specification**  
  Why it matters: describes OpenVEX as minimal, compliant, interoperable, embeddable, and SBOM-agnostic, and explicitly frames VEX as the machine-readable layer scanners can consume to turn off false alerts, which is strong evidence that applicability judgments are being designed to travel as reusable objects rather than remain trapped inside one tool.

- **[S936] Docker Docs — Scanner integrations**  
  Why it matters: says Docker Hardened Images include signed OpenVEX attestations that any VEX-enabled scanner can read, giving scanner flexibility, auditability, and historical visibility, which is unusually direct evidence that vulnerability exceptions are starting to move across tools as reusable standard objects.

- **[S937] Docker Docs — Scan Docker Hardened Images**

- **[S938] Trivy Documentation — Local VEX Files**

- **[S939] Trivy Documentation — VEX Repository**

- **[S940] Trivy Documentation — Vulnerability Exploitability Exchange (VEX) Overview**

## Signer-trust profiles / portable policy bundles

- **[S964] OpenSSF — Signal in the Noise: An Industry-Wide Perspective on the State of VEX**  
  Why it matters: says that beyond signature verification, implementers still face the harder policy problem of deciding whose VEX statement to trust, and that many enterprise consumers still rely on hosting location rather than cryptographic proof, which is unusually direct evidence that trust policy itself is now a first-class bottleneck.

- **[S966] VEX Repository Specification**  
  Why it matters: says clients should support multiple VEX repositories, should select data based on repository priority, and that prioritization should be configurable based on user trust in different data sources, which is direct evidence that portable trust policy is already anticipated by the specification layer.  
  URL: https://github.com/aquasecurity/vex-repo-spec

- **[S967] Aqua Security — VEX Hub**  
  Why it matters: says users should trust package maintainers and their governance rather than VEX Hub itself, and that VEX Hub does not vet the content it aggregates, which is strong evidence that the ecosystem already distinguishes transport convenience from substantive signer trust.  
  URL: https://github.com/aquasecurity/vexhub

- **[S968] OpenVEX Specification — ATTESTING.md**  
  Why it matters: says OpenVEX authors should be cryptographically associated with signatures where possible, but that signing third-party statements implies trusting and adopting those statements into the VEX impact history, which is direct evidence that signing already carries explicit trust-delegation semantics.  
  URL: https://github.com/openvex/spec/blob/main/ATTESTING.md

- **[S969] Sigstore Documentation — Policy Controller Overview**  
  Why it matters: says policy-controller enforces `ClusterImagePolicy` resources with configurable authorities, keyless identities, timestamp authorities, and attestation policies, which is direct evidence that signer trust is already being operationalized as reusable policy objects.

- **[S970] Sigstore Documentation — Sample Policies**  
  Why it matters: publishes concrete `ClusterImagePolicy` examples that require attestations from a custom key or from the public Fulcio root, which is unusually direct evidence that trust profiles already exist as shareable policy bundles rather than only as hidden local settings.  
  URL: https://docs.sigstore.dev/policy-controller/sample-policies/

- **[S971] Docker Docs — Build attestations**  
  Why it matters: says attestations enable policy engines to validate images against policy rules defined by the user, which is direct evidence that signing and attestation are already expected to plug into explicit operator-defined trust policy.  
  URL: https://docs.docker.com/build/metadata/attestations/

- **[S972] Docker Docs — Image validation**  
  Why it matters: frames the practical task as writing policies that validate image inputs, progressing from allowlisting to advanced attestation checks, which is strong evidence that the market is already moving from raw verification to maintained policy expression.

- **[S976] OSV.dev FAQ**

  URL: https://google.github.io/osv.dev/faq/

- **[S977] NVD — CVE-2024-9016 detail**  
  Why it matters: shows a rejected duplicate CVE that explicitly tells users to reference a different CVE instead, which is direct evidence that successor or replacement instructions already appear at the record level and can matter for downstream migration hygiene.  
  URL: https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2024-9016

## Successor-map freshness / service metrics

- **[S987] NVD — Developers Start Here**

- **[S988] OSV.dev — Data sources**

  URL: https://access.redhat.com/articles/5554431

## Trust-bundle translators / interoperability intermediation

- **[S983] Kyverno Documentation — ImageValidatingPolicy**  
  Why it matters: says `ImageValidatingPolicy` is specifically for verifying image signatures and attestations, and that `attestors` declare trusted signing authorities such as keys or certificates, which is direct evidence that Kyverno exposes signer trust as its own policy surface rather than inheriting another tool's model.  
  URL: https://kyverno.io/docs/policy-types/image-validating-policy/

- **[S984] Kyverno Documentation — Sigstore image verification**  
  Why it matters: says each `verifyImages` rule can verify signatures or attestations but not both, and that the `attestors{}` object is used differently depending on whether it appears at the image-signature or attestation layer, which is direct evidence that equivalent trust intent can split differently across policy engines.  
  URL: https://main.kyverno.io/docs/policy-types/cluster-policy/verify-images/sigstore/

- **[S985] Ratify Documentation — Configuration**  
  Why it matters: says Ratify is configured through store, verifier, and executor components and that runtime CRDs can override store and verifier settings from the base configuration file, which is direct evidence that trust and verification intent already move through a different configuration model again.  
  URL: https://ratify.dev/docs/ratify-configuration/

- **[S986] Ratify Documentation — Store/Verifier Providers**  
  Why it matters: says Ratify uses provider models for referrer stores, verifiers, and policy providers and supports both configuration-based and Rego-based policy providers, which is strong evidence that another major enforcement surface expresses trust intent as provider composition rather than repository order or attestor blocks.  
  URL: https://ratify.dev/docs/reference/providers/


## Regression-disclosure windows / governance surface

- **[S990] Docker Docs — Docker Scout Dashboard / Notification settings**  
  Why it matters: says Docker Scout notifications are for newly disclosed upstream vulnerabilities affecting images, not for exposure or policy changes caused by pushing a new image, and that only the last pushed tags can trigger notices, which is direct evidence that reportable-regression scope and timing are already governed rather than left fully ad hoc.

- **[S991] OpenID Foundation — Certification Conformance Testing Disclosure and Reporting Policy**

- **[S992] OpenID Foundation — OpenID Certification**  
  Why it matters: publishes certified implementations by profile and deployment version, which is direct evidence that conformance outcomes already harden into public status surfaces that can lag, update, or be reclassified over time.  
  URL: https://openid.net/certification/


## Translation-loss proofs / audit surface

- **[S993] Trivy Documentation — VEX Overview**  
  Why it matters: says Trivy can enable multiple VEX methods at once and that the order of specification determines priority, which is direct evidence that one enforcement surface already encodes meaning as ordered method precedence rather than as a flat policy import.  
  URL: https://trivy.dev/docs/latest/supply-chain/vex/

- **[S994] Trivy Documentation — VEX Repository**  
  Why it matters: says VEX repository priority is determined by configuration order and that Trivy searches configured repositories in that order until a match is found, which is direct evidence that translation into or out of this model must preserve ordered source precedence.  
  URL: https://trivy.dev/docs/latest/supply-chain/vex/repo/

- **[S995] Sigstore Documentation — Policy Controller Overview**  
  Why it matters: says admission across matched `ClusterImagePolicy` resources is conjunctive, authorities inside a matched policy are disjunctive, and unmatched images can be handled by a separate `no-match-policy` with `warn|allow|deny`, which is strong evidence that another engine expresses similar trust intent with materially different default and matching semantics.  
  URL: https://docs.sigstore.dev/policy-controller/overview/

- **[S996] Kyverno Documentation — Verify Images Overview**  
  Why it matters: says `verifyImages` rules expose `required`, `mutateDigest`, `verifyDigest`, `repository`, and `attestors.count`, and also require certain fields to remain static for deterministic evaluation, which is direct evidence that policy meaning includes defaults and determinism constraints beyond signer selection alone.  
  URL: https://kyverno.io/docs/policy-types/cluster-policy/verify-images/overview/

- **[S997] Kyverno Documentation — Policy Reports**  
  Why it matters: says `verifyImages` and related rules can generate policy reports, which is direct evidence that translated policy behavior is becoming inspectable and auditable rather than only enforced in-line.  
  URL: https://kyverno.io/docs/guides/reports/

- **[S998] Ratify Documentation — Store/Verifier Providers**  
  Why it matters: says config and rego policy providers can return different verification-response versions and that config policy supports artifact-level `any` / `all` success semantics, which is direct evidence that both success logic and report shape can drift across policy models.  
  URL: https://ratify.dev/docs/reference/providers/

- **[S999] Ratify Documentation — Verification Response**  
  Why it matters: says Ratify’s verification response is versioned, that config and rego policies historically produce different response versions, and that the project is tracking format unification, which is unusually direct evidence that response-shape drift is already a recognized interoperability burden.  
  URL: https://ratify.dev/docs/reference/verification-result-version/

- **[S1000] Ratify Documentation — Gatekeeper Policy Authoring**  
  Why it matters: says Rego policies using Ratify external data need to know the structure of the verification response in order to use it correctly, which is direct evidence that downstream policy behavior depends on exact report structure rather than on a vague notion of “verification happened.”  
  URL: https://ratify.dev/docs/quickstarts/gatekeeper-policy-authoring/


## Successor-gap reason codes / operator signals

- **[S1001] NVD — API Transition Guide**  
  Why it matters: says the CPE API now exposes both `deprecatedBy` and `deprecates` relations and that the CVE API now returns `REJECT` / `Rejected` records for a more complete picture, which is direct evidence that deprecation handoffs and non-active records are already first-class machine-readable states rather than invisible edge cases.  
  URL: https://nvd.nist.gov/general/news/api-20-announcements

- **[S1002] NVD — Vulnerability APIs**  
  Why it matters: names `CPE Deprecation Remap` and `CVE Rejected` as explicit change-history events and says rejection can happen for duplicate entries, withdrawal by the original requester, incorrect assignment, or other administrative reasons, which is strong evidence that unresolved or invalid handoffs already fall into distinct operational classes.  
  URL: https://nvd.nist.gov/developers/vulnerabilities

- **[S1003] CVE Program — Frequently Asked Questions**  
  Why it matters: says REJECT reasons are typically stated in the record description, gives examples such as duplicate, withdrawal, incorrect assignment, or administrative reason, and notes that rejected records can later move back to a published state, which is direct evidence that “do not use this ID” is not a single stable meaning.  
  URL: https://www.cve.org/Resources/Media/Archives/OldWebsite/about/faqs.html

- **[S1004] Open Source Vulnerability Schema**  
  Why it matters: distinguishes `aliases`, `upstream`, and `related` as different relationship types and explicitly forbids using aliases for upstream/downstream supply-chain relationships, which is direct evidence that adjacent records can be linked in materially different ways rather than through a single generic successor edge.  
  URL: https://ossf.github.io/osv-schema/

- **[S1005] OSV.dev — Frequently Asked Questions**  
  Why it matters: says withdrawn records remain retrievable but are excluded from main query surfaces, while deleted records may be converted to withdrawn or left valid-but-orphaned depending on source type, which is unusually direct evidence that missing or non-primary records already occupy multiple machine-relevant states.  
  URL: https://google.github.io/osv.dev/faq/

- **[S1006] Red Hat Security Data Changelog**  
  Why it matters: says deleted CSAF / VEX files are tracked in `deletions.csv`, are removed when source data changes or becomes invalid, and should be purged from downstream systems, which is direct evidence that “there is no live successor here” can already mean stale-or-invalid rather than merely delayed.  
  URL: https://access.redhat.com/articles/5554431

- **[S1007] Red Hat Security Data — CSAF Advisories directory**  
  Why it matters: publishes `changes.csv`, `deletions.csv`, `releases.csv`, and `archive_latest.txt` as separate machine-readable surfaces, which is strong evidence that live change, deletion, release, and archive status are already operationally distinct states for consumers.  
  URL: https://security.access.redhat.com/data/csaf/v2/advisories/

- **[S1008] NVD — Deprecated Products API**  
  Why it matters: says deprecated CPEs are not returned by default, explains that deprecation can arise from correction or product-name evolution, and models `deprecated` / `deprecatedBy` explicitly in returned records, which is direct evidence that replacement state already needs surrounding interpretation by downstream consumers.  
  URL: https://nvd.nist.gov/developers/products-1

- **[S1011] Dependency-Track Documentation — Auditing Basics**

  Why it matters: says rejected CVE records remain visible so users know the record is invalid and should no longer be used, which is direct evidence that retired negative states may remain historically visible rather than disappearing outright.

- **[S1013] Red Hat Quay / Clair Documentation — Vulnerability reporting with Clair on Red Hat Quay**  
  Why it matters: says Clair’s notifier informs users when new or removed vulnerabilities affect an indexed manifest, which is unusually direct evidence that disappearance of a vulnerability is already treated as a meaningful event requiring refreshed evidence.

## Semantic-equivalence grades / migration shorthand

- **[S1015] Trivy Documentation — VEX Overview**  
  Why it matters: says multiple VEX methods can be enabled simultaneously and that the order of specification determines priority, which is direct evidence that one policy surface already encodes meaning as ordered method precedence rather than as a flat import.  
  URL: https://trivy.dev/docs/latest/supply-chain/vex/

- **[S1016] Trivy Documentation — VEX Repository**  
  Why it matters: says configured repository order determines search priority, that search stops at the first match, and that the first matching VEX document determines final status, which is direct evidence that translation can change policy meaning by changing source order alone.  
  URL: https://trivy.dev/docs/latest/supply-chain/vex/repo/

- **[S1017] Sigstore Documentation — Policy Controller Overview**  
  Why it matters: says matched `ClusterImagePolicy` resources all need to pass, that at least one authority in a matched policy must pass, and that unmatched images are governed separately by configurable `no-match-policy` behavior with a deny default when unspecified, which is strong evidence that another engine expresses similar trust intent through materially different defaults and matching semantics.  
  URL: https://docs.sigstore.dev/policy-controller/overview/

- **[S1018] Kyverno Documentation — Verify Images Overview**  
  Why it matters: says `verifyImages` policy meaning includes `required`, `mutateDigest`, `verifyDigest`, alternate signature repositories, and attestor counts, which is direct evidence that equivalence across engines depends on defaults and enforcement structure, not just signer lists.

- **[S1019] Kyverno Documentation — Policy Reports**  
  Why it matters: says `verifyImages` results are emitted as policy reports for currently matching resources and that reports always represent current state rather than retained history, which is direct evidence that translation can also change what downstream audit surface exists after enforcement.

- **[S1020] Ratify Documentation — Store/Verifier Providers**  
  Why it matters: says verification results differ between Rego and config policy providers, that Rego uses verification-response `1.0.0`, and that config policy supports per-artifact `any` / `all` success logic with configurable defaults, which is unusually direct evidence that response shape and success semantics themselves are part of what must be graded during migration.  
  URL: https://ratify.dev/docs/reference/providers/

- **[S1021] OSV — Properties of a High Quality OSV Record**

- **[S1022] NVD — Product APIs**

- **[S1023] OpenID Foundation — OpenID Federation Subordinate Events Endpoint 1.0**

- **[S1024] NVD — Data Feeds**

- **[S1027] SLSA — About SLSA**

- **[S1035] Basel Convention — Notification & Movement Documents**

- **[S1036] European Commission — Implementation of the Waste Shipment Regulation**  
  Why it matters: says DIWASS will be used from 21 May 2026 for the electronic submission and exchange of waste-shipment documents and information, including movement documents and Annex VII documents, which is direct evidence that waste-routing paperwork is becoming digital infrastructure.

- **[S1038] European Commission — Commission adopts key legal act to simplify and digitalise waste shipments in the EU**

  URL: https://environment.ec.europa.eu/news/commission-adopts-key-legal-act-digitalise-eu-waste-shipments-2025-07-02_en



## Financial assurance / phased release / closure evidence packs

- **[S1039] OSMRE — Reclamation Bonds**  
  Why it matters: says permittees may apply for partial bond release as reclamation is completed and that release is organized into three phases, which is direct evidence that liability reduction already depends on staged evidentiary milestones rather than one final closure event.  
  URL: https://www.osmre.gov/resources/reclamation-bonds

- **[S1040] U.S. EPA — Financial Assurance Requirements for Hazardous Waste Treatment, Storage and Disposal Facilities**  
  Why it matters: says all hazardous-waste treatment, storage, and disposal facilities must demonstrate financial resources for closure and, where applicable, post-closure care, with cost estimates based on paying a third party and adjusted annually, which is strong evidence that closure liability is already pre-financed and evidence-sensitive.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/hwpermitting/financial-assurance-requirements-hazardous-waste-treatment-storage-and-disposal

- **[S1041] U.S. EPA — Closure and Post-Closure Care Requirements for Hazardous Waste Treatment, Storage and Disposal Facilities**  
  Why it matters: says hazardous-waste facilities must close on a strict timetable, certify closure through an independent professional engineer, and continue post-closure monitoring and maintenance where waste remains in place, which is direct evidence that closure is a formal milestone-plus-monitoring regime rather than a casual endpoint.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/hwpermitting/closure-and-post-closure-care-requirements-hazardous-waste-treatment-storage-and

- **[S1042] U.S. EPA — Requirements for Municipal Solid Waste Landfills (MSWLFs)**  
  Why it matters: says landfill operators must certify closure, maintain a written post-closure care plan, perform a required 30-year post-closure care period unless modified, and demonstrate financial assurance for closure, post-closure, and corrective action, which is strong evidence that the long tail after nominal closure is already governed and financed.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/landfills/requirements-municipal-solid-waste-landfills-mswlfs

- **[S1043] U.S. EPA — Class VI Wells Used for Geologic Sequestration of Carbon Dioxide**  
  Why it matters: says Class VI operators must maintain financial responsibility for corrective action, plugging, post-injection site care, and emergency response and must keep monitoring through the life of the project until no additional monitoring is needed, which is strong evidence that site closure and capital release increasingly depend on monitored proof over time.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/uic/class-vi-wells-used-geologic-sequestration-carbon-dioxide

- **[S1044] U.S. EPA — Final Class VI Guidance Documents**  
  Why it matters: shows EPA maintaining dedicated guidance for well plugging, post-injection site care, site closure, reporting, and financial responsibility across the lifecycle of a Class VI project, which reinforces that closure proof and financial assurance are now a sustained program layer rather than an afterthought.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/uic/final-class-vi-guidance-documents

- **[S1045] NRC — Financial Assurance for Decommissioning**  
  Why it matters: says many licensees must prepare site-specific cost estimates, decommissioning funding plans, and maintain financial assurance before contamination-generating operations begin, which is direct evidence that closure liability is provisioned in advance and managed as a live financial object.  
  URL: https://www.nrc.gov/waste/decommissioning/finan-assur

- **[S1046] NRC — 10 CFR § 30.36 Expiration and Termination of Licenses and Decommissioning of Sites**  
  Why it matters: says financial assurance must be maintained during decommissioning and may be decreased only as decommissioning proceeds and contamination is reduced with Commission approval, which is unusually direct evidence that release of reserved capital follows phased evidentiary review.  
  URL: https://www.nrc.gov/reading-rm/doc-collections/cfr/part030/part030-0036

- **[S1047] NRC — 10 CFR § 50.82 Termination of License**  
  Why it matters: says reactor licensees must submit decommissioning activity reports, site-specific cost estimates, and annual financial-assurance status reports until they complete the final radiation survey and demonstrate conditions for license termination, which is strong evidence that high-stakes closure is a documented multi-stage release process.  
  URL: https://www.nrc.gov/reading-rm/doc-collections/cfr/part050/part050-0082

- **[S1048] BOEM — Renewable Energy Guidance**  
  Why it matters: says BOEM’s updated renewable-energy financial-assurance regulations better reflect the risks of decommissioning offshore renewable facilities, which shows that large infrastructure exit is increasingly governed as a live financial-risk and evidence problem rather than a distant cleanup footnote.  
  URL: https://www.boem.gov/oil-gas-energy/risk-management/renewable-energy-guidance

- **[S1049] BOEM — Financial Assurance Requirements for the Offshore Oil and Gas Industry Operating on the OCS**  
  Why it matters: frames financial assurance as ensuring sufficient resources for decommissioning and liability obligations and explicitly ties the regime to protecting taxpayers from those burdens, which reinforces that closure proof is increasingly inseparable from capital-reserve governance.  
  URL: https://www.boem.gov/oil-gas-energy/risk-management/financial-assurance-requirements-offshore-oil-and-gas-industry


## Post-closure monitoring / long-term stewardship / continuity markets

- **[S1050] eCFR — 40 CFR 264.117 Post-closure care and use of property**  
  Why it matters: says post-closure care for covered hazardous-waste units must continue for 30 years after closure and includes monitoring, reporting, and maintenance of containment systems, which is direct evidence that nominal closure often opens into a long supervised tail rather than ending obligations.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-I/part-264/subpart-G/section-264.117

- **[S1051] eCFR — 40 CFR 264.144 Cost estimate for post-closure care**  
  Why it matters: says hazardous-waste post-closure cost estimates must be based on hiring a third party to conduct post-closure activities, which is unusually direct evidence that continuity of care is already framed as something another actor may need to assume.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-I/part-264/subpart-H/section-264.144

- **[S1052] eCFR — 40 CFR 258.61 Post-closure care requirements**  
  Why it matters: says municipal solid-waste landfill post-closure care must generally continue for 30 years, which is direct evidence that even ordinary waste infrastructure is governed through long monitoring and maintenance windows after formal closure.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-I/part-258/subpart-F/section-258.61

- **[S1053] eCFR — 40 CFR Part 258 Subpart G Financial Assurance Criteria**  
  Why it matters: says landfill operators must estimate the cost of hiring a third party for post-closure care and maintain continuous financial assurance until formally released, which is strong evidence that the real bottleneck is continuity of supervised care rather than one-time closure alone.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-I/part-258/subpart-G

- **[S1054] eCFR — 40 CFR 146.93 Post-injection site care and site closure**  
  Why it matters: says Class VI operators must continue monitoring for at least 50 years after cessation of injection unless an alternative timeframe is approved and must keep caring for the site if they cannot yet prove monitoring is no longer needed, which is direct evidence that some modern infrastructure sectors are designed around very long supervised tails.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-146/subpart-H/section-146.93

- **[S1055] U.S. DOE — Long-Term Stewardship: Lessons Learned**  
  Why it matters: defines long-term stewardship to include land-use controls, monitoring, maintenance, and information management after cleanup, which is direct evidence that post-closure continuity is already understood as a durable governance function rather than a temporary afterthought.  
  URL: https://www.energy.gov/lm/long-term-stewardship-lessons-learned

- **[S1056] U.S. DOE — About OSO (LM-20)**  
  Why it matters: says DOE’s Office of Site Operations already conducts long-term surveillance and maintenance at more than 100 sites in over 30 states and with 25 tribes, with additional transitions ahead, which is strong evidence that supervised tails can mature into a standing organizational field.  
  URL: https://www.energy.gov/lm/about-oso-lm-20


## Land-use controls / deed notices / shadow zoning

- **[S1057] U.S. EPA — Superfund: Institutional Controls**  
  Why it matters: says institutional controls are non-engineered administrative and legal controls that limit land or resource use, guide human behavior, and are used where residual contamination remains above levels allowing unlimited use and unrestricted exposure, which is direct evidence that some parcels already operate under long-lived restricted-use constitutions.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/superfund/superfund-institutional-controls

- **[S1058] eCFR — 40 CFR 264.119 Post-closure notices**  
  Why it matters: requires hazardous-waste disposal units to record deed or title-search notations in perpetuity and file waste-location records with land-use authorities, which is unusually direct evidence that post-closure restrictions are encoded into property-transfer infrastructure.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-I/part-264/subpart-G/section-264.119

- **[S1059] eCFR — 40 CFR Part 258 Subpart F Closure and Post-Closure Care**  
  Why it matters: requires landfill deed notations in perpetuity, post-closure plans, named contact points, and planned-use descriptions, which shows ordinary waste infrastructure already generating durable use restrictions tied to property records and stewardship plans.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-I/part-258/subpart-F

- **[S1060] eCFR — 40 CFR 146.93 Post-injection site care and site closure**  
  Why it matters: requires Class VI carbon-sequestration sites to file plats, record deed notices in perpetuity, retain post-closure records, and transfer those records to the regulator, which is strong evidence that subsurface projects can leave long-lived place-governance layers after active operations end.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-146/subpart-H/section-146.93

- **[S1061] NRC — 10 CFR 20.1403 Criteria for license termination under restricted conditions**  
  Why it matters: requires legally enforceable institutional controls and financial assurance for an independent third party or governmental custodian when a site is terminated under restricted use, which is direct evidence that some high-stakes sites remain governable only through durable control-and-custody layers.  
  URL: https://www.nrc.gov/reading-rm/doc-collections/cfr/part020/part020-1403

- **[S1062] U.S. EPA — Superfund (CERCLA) Compliance Monitoring**  
  Why it matters: says EPA seeks to hold responsible parties accountable for implementing and maintaining institutional controls and works with states and local governments to monitor compliance, which is direct evidence that restricted-use remedies create a continuing enforcement layer after cleanup decisions are made.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/compliance/superfund-cercla-compliance-monitoring

- **[S1063] EPA Office of Inspector General — Evaluation of Institutional Control Documentation in the Superfund Enterprise Management System on IIJA Funded Sites**  
  Why it matters: found incomplete and inaccurate institutional-control data limiting oversight, reporting, public awareness, and meaningful decision-making, which is strong evidence that discoverability and maintenance of this layer are already operational bottlenecks.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/sites/default/files/oig/documents/_epaoig_20250319-25-e-0020_cert.pdf

- **[S1064] U.S. DOE — Long-Term Stewardship: Lessons Learned**  
  Why it matters: defines long-term stewardship to include land-use controls, monitoring, maintenance, and information management, which shows that restricted-use places already mature into standing stewardship programs rather than one-off cleanup endings.  
  URL: https://www.energy.gov/lm/long-term-stewardship-lessons-learned

- **[S1065] U.S. DOE — Long-Term Stewardship Resource Center**  
  Why it matters: says long-term stewardship procedures combine land-use controls, monitoring and maintenance, and information management under multiple statutes, which reinforces that conditioned land is governed through an enduring cross-law coordination layer.  
  URL: https://www.energy.gov/lm/long-term-stewardship-resource-center


## Restriction-search infrastructure / conveyancing / lookup layers

- **[S1066] eCFR — 40 CFR Part 312 Innocent Landowners, Standards for Conducting All Appropriate Inquiries**  
  Why it matters: requires review of government records and databases including registries or publicly available lists of engineering controls and institutional controls applicable to a subject property, which is direct evidence that restriction lookup is already part of baseline acquisition diligence.

- **[S1067] U.S. EPA — Superfund Site Information (example Activity and Use Limitations page)**

- **[S1068] Pennsylvania DEP — Uniform Environmental Covenants**  
  Why it matters: says Pennsylvania requires an environmental covenant whenever an engineering or institutional control is used to demonstrate attainment of an Act 2 remediation standard, which is direct evidence that durable land-use restrictions are being formalized as standard remediation instruments rather than rare exceptions.  
  URL: https://www.pa.gov/agencies/dep/programs-and-services/land/land-recycling-program/uniform-environmental-covenants

- **[S1069] Pennsylvania DEP — PA AUL Registry**

- **[S1070] New York State DEC — Environmental Site Database Search**  
  Why it matters: says New York’s searchable remediation database is updated nightly, includes institutional and engineering controls, and offers GIS files for site boundaries, which is strong evidence that environmental restriction lookup is already being built into searchable parcel-data workflows.  
  URL: https://dec.ny.gov/environmental-protection/site-cleanup/database-search

- **[S1071] U.S. EPA — Contaminated Site Locations and Contact Information**  
  Why it matters: points users to multiple contaminated-site mapper viewers across cleanup programs, which is good evidence that the practical lookup layer is already distributed across many program-specific surfaces rather than resolved into one unified search system.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/risks-contaminated-sites/contaminated-site-locations-and-contact-information


## Machine-readable restriction objects / transaction middleware

- **[S1072] U.S. EPA — E-Enterprise for the Environment**  
  Why it matters: says EPA, states, and tribes are modernizing environmental protection by streamlining business processes and leveraging smarter technology, which is a direct signal that environmental governance is being pushed toward more reusable digital operating surfaces.

- **[S1073] U.S. EPA — Data Documentation for Mapping and Screening Criteria for Renewable Energy Generation Potential on EPA- and State-Tracked Sites**  
  Why it matters: says state-tracked contaminated-site datasets vary in detail and that EPA aims to standardize the reported data gathered across a wide range of states, which is unusually direct evidence that multi-state contaminated-land governance is running into interoperability and field-normalization pressure.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/re-powering/5-data-documentation-mapping-and-screening-criteria-renewable-energy-generation

- **[S1074] California DTSC — Your EnviroStor**  
  Why it matters: presents EnviroStor as a public search-and-download surface for thousands of cleanup and hazardous-waste sites, which is good evidence that state programs increasingly expect contaminated-site information to function as reusable digital data rather than file-room-only records.  
  URL: https://dtsc.ca.gov/your-envirostor/

- **[S1075] California DTSC — Land Use Restriction Sites**  
  Why it matters: defines a land-use-restricted site as property whose future use is limited or conditioned because of cleanup realities, which is direct evidence that conditioned parcels are being maintained as an explicit public category rather than buried inside narrative case files.  
  URL: https://dtsc.ca.gov/land-use-restriction-sites/

- **[S1076] New York State DEC — DECinfo Locator Layers**  
  Why it matters: publishes environmental-easement and parcel-facing layer information with update frequencies, which shows that some programs are already exposing land-governance constraints as map layers designed to be routinely activated, viewed, and cross-referenced rather than discovered only through bespoke inquiry.
