## Action clearance / excavation safety / work-order gating

- **[S1077] OSHA — 29 CFR 1926.651 Specific Excavation Requirements**  
  Why it matters: requires locating underground installations before excavation, contacting utilities or owners, and using safe means to determine exact locations, which is direct evidence that ordinary field work already depends on action-specific pre-clearance rather than raw map knowledge alone.  
  URL: https://www.osha.gov/laws-regs/regulations/standardnumber/1926/1926.651

- **[S1078] PHMSA — 811 Day**  
  Why it matters: says excavation damage remains the leading cause of pipeline incidents and frames calling 811 as the foremost preventive measure, which is unusually direct evidence that clearance-before-action has already become core safety infrastructure.  
  URL: https://www.phmsa.dot.gov/safety-awareness/811-day

- **[S1079] PHMSA — Call Before You Dig**  
  Why it matters: says every digging project requires contact with the one-call center before digging and that facility operators must determine whether underground infrastructure may be affected, which shows the clearance object already functioning as a mass operational gateway.  
  URL: https://primis.phmsa.dot.gov/comm/cbyd.htm

- **[S1080] U.S. EPA — How Superfund Addresses Groundwater Contamination**  
  Why it matters: lists building or excavation permits and well-drilling prohibitions as common institutional controls, which is direct evidence that residual-risk governance often operates by intercepting proposed actions rather than merely posting static notices.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/superfund/how-superfund-addresses-groundwater-contamination

- **[S1081] U.S. EPA — Stormwater Discharges from Construction Activities**  
  Why it matters: says earth-disturbing construction activities including clearing, grading, and excavating land can require permit coverage, which shows that routine field work frequently crosses explicit authorization thresholds before it may proceed.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/npdes/stormwater-discharges-construction-activities

- **[S1082] New York State Department of Public Service — Underground Damage Prevention**  
  Why it matters: shows a current state-level one-call regime with mandatory notice of intent to excavate and excavator training, which reinforces that action clearance is not niche but ordinary operating infrastructure.  
  URL: https://dps.ny.gov/underground-damage-prevention

- **[S1083] eCFR — 40 CFR 312.26 Reviews of Federal, State, Tribal, and local government records**  
  Why it matters: requires all-appropriate-inquiries review of permits, waste-management records, and public lists of engineering and institutional controls applicable to a subject property, which shows that the underlying clearance stack already spans many records and registries.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-J/part-312/subpart-C/section-312.26

- **[S1084] U.S. EPA — Contaminated Site Locations and Contact Information**  
  Why it matters: routes users across multiple cleanup-program viewers and notes that information availability varies by site and cleanup scope, which is strong evidence that any action-clearance layer must cope with fragmented source surfaces rather than one complete registry.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/risks-contaminated-sites/contaminated-site-locations-and-contact-information

- **[S1085] U.S. EPA — Data Documentation for Mapping and Screening Criteria for Renewable Energy Generation Potential on EPA- and State-Tracked Sites**  
  Why it matters: says the public mapper covers only a subset of nationwide contaminated lands, includes state snapshots, and may change over time, which is direct evidence that operational work clearance will often need freshness checks above incomplete or time-bounded source layers.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/re-powering/5-data-documentation-mapping-and-screening-criteria-renewable-energy-generation


## Replay-grade evidence / one-call logging / permit records

- **[S1086] Pennsylvania One Call System — User Guide Text**  
  Why it matters: says ticket confirmations include the serial number, ticket information, and facility owners notified; treats the serial number as proof of notification that should be saved; and requires facility owners to respond through the system by the response due date, which is direct evidence that ordinary clearance already produces evidentiary objects rather than informal conversations alone.  
  URL: https://www.pa1call.org/pocs/68a17ede-7779-4ad3-afae-653ace1e1e8a/User-Guide-Text

- **[S1087] Pennsylvania — Underground Utility Line Protection Law**  
  Why it matters: requires deadline-bound responses through the One Call System, digital logging of the full voice transaction, five-year retention of those logs, indexed record availability, investigation access to response records and tickets, and even a secure repository for subsurface utility engineering data, which is unusually clear evidence that clearance is hardening into a replayable record system.  
  URL: https://www.pa1call.org/resourcelibrary/resource/download?id=215

- **[S1088] eCFR — 40 CFR 122.41 Conditions applicable to all permits**  
  Why it matters: requires retention of monitoring information, calibration and maintenance records, permit reports, and the data used to complete the permit application for at least three years and makes those records available to regulators on request, which shows permit-governed work already assumes later record replay and inspection.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-122/subpart-C/section-122.41

- **[S1089] U.S. EPA — Construction General Permit Resources, Tools, and Templates**  
  Why it matters: EPA publishes standardized templates for site inspections, dewatering inspections, and corrective-action logs specifically to document required findings, which shows routine field compliance is already being normalized into reusable evidence forms.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/npdes/construction-general-permit-resources-tools-and-templates

- **[S1090] U.S. EPA — Inspection and Monitoring Guide for Construction Dewatering (2022 CGP)**  
  Why it matters: recommends regularly transferring inspection and monitoring information into databases or spreadsheets, allows electronic corrective-action logs only if they remain legally dependable and immediately accessible, and requires retention of complete corrective-action logs for at least three years after permit coverage ends, which is direct evidence that replay-grade compliance records are already expected.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/system/files/documents/2022-01/cgp-inspection-and-monitoring-guide-for-dewatering.pdf

- **[S1091] U.S. EPA — Electronic Reporting for EPA's NPDES General Permits**  
  Why it matters: says electronic reporting is required for construction sites and other general-permit contexts, which is direct evidence that front-end authorization artifacts are being pulled into retained digital reporting systems rather than left as isolated paperwork.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/npdes/electronic-reporting-epas-npdes-general-permits

- **[S1092] U.S. EPA — Submitting NOI / NOT / LEW under the Construction General Permit**  
  Why it matters: shows CGP notices of intent, termination, and modification are submitted through EPA’s CGP-NeT e-reporting system, which reinforces that clearance-state artifacts are increasingly born digital and easier to retain, query, and replay.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/npdes/submitting-notice-intent-noi-notice-termination-not-or-low-erosivity-waiver-lew-under

- **[S1093] PHMSA — Stakeholder Communications: Damage Prevention**  
  Why it matters: says excavation damage continues to be a leading cause of pipeline incidents, which is the live operational pressure pushing apparently minor ticketing and response artifacts into serious post-incident evidence.  
  URL: https://primis.phmsa.dot.gov/comm/damageprevention.htm


## Source-snapshot escrow / custody / live-source drift

- **[S1094] OSHA — 29 CFR 1926.651 Specific Excavation Requirements**  
  Why it matters: requires determining the estimated location of underground utilities before excavation, contacting utility owners, and safely establishing exact locations as work approaches them, which shows that ordinary field decisions already depend on concrete external source states rather than on generic permission alone.  
  URL: https://www.osha.gov/laws-regs/regulations/standardnumber/1926/1926.651

- **[S1095] Pennsylvania One Call System — Underground Utility Line Protection Law (current text)**  
  Why it matters: requires digital logging of one-call transactions for five years, indexed record access, and maintenance of existing written or electronic records or drawings for abandoned main lines, which is unusually direct evidence that decision-state reconstruction depends on preserving source records and not just ticket numbers.  
  URL: https://www.pa1call.org/pocs/7bf4a38e-2dbf-43ce-89b2-aa1f03b2352e/PA-Act-287-as-amended

- **[S1096] U.S. EPA — Construction General Permit Frequent Questions**  
  Why it matters: says electronic compliance records should preserve metadata, detect modifications, identify a separate custodian of records, keep originals in one protected location, and remain demonstrably unchanged after migration to a successor recordkeeping system, which is near-direct evidence for escrow-grade custody design.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/npdes/construction-general-permit-cgp-frequent-questions

- **[S1097] eCFR — 40 CFR 122.41 Conditions applicable to all permits**  
  Why it matters: requires retention of monitoring information, reports, and the data used to complete permit applications for at least three years and makes those records available to regulators on request, which shows permit-governed work already assumes later replay of the underlying decision material.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-122/subpart-C/section-122.41

- **[S1098] U.S. EPA — Data Documentation for Mapping and Screening Criteria for Renewable Energy Generation Potential on EPA- and State-Tracked Sites**  
  Why it matters: says the mapper data are snapshots in time, that site-specific information may change over time, and that included state datasets are only a subset of nationwide contaminated lands, which is direct evidence that many operational source surfaces are time-bounded and incomplete.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/re-powering/5-data-documentation-mapping-and-screening-criteria-renewable-energy-generation

- **[S1099] Pennsylvania DEP — AUL Disclaimer**  
  Why it matters: says AUL locations are approximate, some sites are not included, the registry is informational, and DEP may change the information at any time, which is unusually direct evidence that current-state lookup layers are too unstable to substitute for preserved decision-time snapshots.  
  URL: https://gis.dep.pa.gov/pa-aul/Links/AUL%20DISCLAIMER.htm

- **[S1100] New York State DEC — Environmental Site Database Search**  
  Why it matters: says remediation, spill, and bulk-storage databases are updated nightly and paired with GIS outputs, which shows that even useful official source systems are moving targets unless their decision-time state is preserved.  
  URL: https://dec.ny.gov/environmental-protection/site-cleanup/database-search

- **[S1101] National Archives — Digital Preservation Strategy 2022-2026**  
  Why it matters: says authenticity depends on documented preservation actions and that preservation metadata should keep essential contextual, administrative, descriptive, and technical information with the record, which is strong evidence that later admissibility depends on preserved context and not just preserved content.  
  URL: https://www.archives.gov/preservation/digital-preservation/strategy

- **[S1102] U.S. EPA — Contaminated Site Locations and Contact Information**  
  Why it matters: routes users across multiple cleanup-program viewers and says information availability varies by site and cleanup scope, which shows that the decision-state substrate is often fragmented across many source surfaces rather than preserved in one authoritative pane.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/risks-contaminated-sites/contaminated-site-locations-and-contact-information

- **[S1103] National Archives — Context for Electronic Records Management**  
  Why it matters: says electronic recordkeeping should preserve not only content but also context and structure over time, which is a concise official statement of why a raw file or log alone is too weak for durable replay.  
  URL: https://www.archives.gov/records-mgmt/initiatives/context-for-erm.html

- **[S1104] National Archives — NARA Transfer Guidance**  
  Why it matters: says agencies must transfer documentation adequate for NARA to identify, service, and interpret permanent electronic records for as long as they are needed, which supports the broader claim that later interpretability must be deliberately preserved.  
  URL: https://www.archives.gov/records-mgmt/policy/transfer-guidance.html

- **[S1105] National Archives — Metadata Requirements for Permanent Electronic Records**  
  Why it matters: consolidates current metadata requirements for permanent electronic records and stresses that transfers must comply with NARA requirements, which reinforces that preservation-grade records are inseparable from their metadata bundles.  
  URL: https://www.archives.gov/records-mgmt/policy/metadata-compiled

- **[S1110] New York State DEC — Environmental Site Database Search**

## Reliance-scope matrices / transaction-class exhibits

  Why it matters: says the Standard Flood Hazard Determination Form is required for all federally backed loans, which is direct evidence that one transaction class already depends on a named determination object rather than on general map visibility alone.

- **[S1115] eCFR — 12 CFR 22.6 Required use of standard flood hazard determination form**

- **[S1116] U.S. Army Corps of Engineers, Philadelphia District — Jurisdictional Determinations**  
  Why it matters: describes an Approved Jurisdictional Determination as an official Corps determination, valid for five years and appealable, which is unusually direct evidence that waters questions already use a scope-bound official reliance object.

- **[S1117] NRCS — Certified Wetlands Determination**  
  Why it matters: says a certified wetland determination identifies areas subject to wetland-conservation provisions and stays in effect while the land remains in agricultural use unless review is requested after error or hydrologic change, which shows a second official determination product with a different scope and validity logic.

- **[S1118] NRCS — Conservation Compliance for Wetlands**  
  Why it matters: explains that producers request wetland determinations to preserve eligibility for USDA programs and that drainage, filling, clearing, land leveling, or excavation can trigger renewed review, which shows transaction-class and activity-class dependence rather than generic site truth.

- **[S1119] FEMA Flood Map Service Center**  
  Why it matters: describes the MSC as FEMA’s official public source for NFIP flood-hazard information while warning that effective information may change or become superseded over time, which is strong evidence that official map access and durable lending-grade reliance are not identical objects.  
  URL: https://msc.fema.gov/

## Re-review trigger grammars / redetermination conditions

- **[S1120] eCFR — 40 CFR 312.20 All appropriate inquiries**  
  Why it matters: requires all appropriate inquiries within one year before acquisition, requires several components to be conducted or updated within 180 days, and requires previously collected information to be updated for relevant changes in conditions, which is an official example of a named freshness and refresh grammar.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-J/part-312/subpart-C/section-312.20

- **[S1121] U.S. Army Corps of Engineers — January 5, 2022 Navigable Waters Protection Rule Vacatur announcement**  
  Why it matters: states that Approved Jurisdictional Determinations are generally valid for five years unless new information warrants revision prior to expiration, which is unusually direct evidence that one official land/waters determination already uses explicit pre-expiry revision triggers.  
  URL: https://www.usace.army.mil/Media/Announcements/Article/2888988/5-january-2022-navigable-waters-protection-rule-vacatur/

- **[S1122] U.S. Army Corps of Engineers, Philadelphia District — Jurisdictional Determinations**  
  Why it matters: describes an approved JD as an official Corps determination, valid for five years and appealable, which helps anchor the time-based part of the Corps trigger grammar in a current public-facing page.  
  URL: https://www.nap.usace.army.mil/Missions/Regulatory/Jurisdictional-Determinations/

- **[S1123] NRCS — Certified Wetlands Determination**  
  Why it matters: says a certified wetland determination stays in effect while the land remains in agricultural use unless the producer requests review after an error or a natural event that altered site hydrology, which is a compact official example of event-class-based re-review logic.  
  URL: https://www.nrcs.usda.gov/resources/guides-and-instructions/certified-wetlands-determination

- **[S1124] NRCS — Conservation Compliance: Highly Erodible Lands and Wetlands Provisions**  
  Why it matters: says AD-1026 self-certification remains effective unless agricultural operations change or new activities arise, such as bringing new land into production or installing new drainage structures, which shows activity-change triggers rather than mere calendar expiry.  
  URL: https://www.nrcs.usda.gov/getting-assistance/financial-help/conservation-compliance

- **[S1125] FEMA Flood Map Service Center**  
  Why it matters: says the MSC is the official public source for NFIP flood-hazard information but warns that downloaded or printed effective information may change or become superseded by new maps over time, which is direct evidence that official source access and stable reuse are different things.  
  URL: https://msc.fema.gov/

- **[S1126] FEMA — Flood Hazard Mapping Updates Overview Fact Sheet**  
  Why it matters: says flood hazards are dynamic, maps are periodically updated, new or revised maps can change hazard and building or insurance requirements, and revalidation letters identify previously issued LOMCs that remain valid after a revised FIRM, which makes FEMA an unusually clear public example of supersession-plus-revalidation logic.  
  URL: https://msc.fema.gov/msccontent/Flood_Hazard_Mapping_Updates_Overview_Fact_Sheet.pdf

## Emergency overrides / break-glass constitutions

- **[S1127] OSHA — 29 CFR 1926.651 Specific Excavation Requirements**  
  Why it matters: requires utility owners to be contacted before excavation, but also allows cautious proceed rights with detection equipment when utilities cannot respond within 24 hours or cannot establish the exact location, which is a compact federal example of a bounded proceed-under-uncertainty rule rather than a pure stop rule.  
  URL: https://www.osha.gov/laws-regs/regulations/standardnumber/1926/1926.651

- **[S1128] Pennsylvania 811 — User Guide Text, Emergency Notices**  
  Why it matters: defines emergency excavation in operational terms, requires the excavator to describe the emergency, and says the ticket will be queued for immediate transmission, which is unusually direct evidence that emergency claims are already governed through named intake and routing rules rather than informal discretion.  
  URL: https://www.pa1call.org/pocs/04b6de25-21d2-4dbf-a50b-2b81413a9bbf/User-Guide-Text

- **[S1129] Pennsylvania One Call — Underground Utility Line Protection Law (Act 287, as amended)**  
  Why it matters: defines emergency, requires emergency responses as soon as practicable, relaxes ordinary timing in emergencies while preserving notification duties, penalizes misrepresentation of emergency excavation, and permits limited proceed rights after failed direct contact, which makes it a rich public example of an actual override constitution.  
  URL: https://www.pa1call.org/pocs/7bf4a38e-2dbf-43ce-89b2-aa1f03b2352e/PA-Act-287-as-amended

- **[S1130] Pennsylvania 811 — Excavators, Direct Contact Rule**  
  Why it matters: says that when a site is unmarked or incorrectly marked on initial arrival, the excavator renotifies, the facility owner has two hours to make direct contact, and excavation may begin after three hours if sufficient information is still not provided, which is a concrete example of fallback authority with explicit clocks and due-care obligations.  
  URL: https://www.pa1call.org/pocs/350849e1-ae0e-4361-b513-21b28fe3b9a0/Excavators

- **[S1131] eCFR — 33 CFR 325.2 Processing of applications, emergency procedures**  
  Why it matters: authorizes special Corps permit-processing procedures when waiting for standard timelines would create hazard, loss, or immediate economic hardship, while still requiring reasonable efforts to receive comments and publish the rationale, which shows emergency process compression without total process disappearance.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-33/chapter-II/part-325/section-325.2

- **[S1132] eCFR — 40 CFR 270.61 Emergency permits**  
  Why it matters: allows EPA or authorized states to issue temporary emergency hazardous-waste permits, including oral permits later reduced to writing, with explicit duration, scope, and public-notice requirements, which is a mature example of a tightly bounded break-glass authorization object.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-I/part-270/subpart-F/section-270.61

- **[S1133] eCFR — 44 CFR 59.3 Emergency program**  
  Why it matters: says FEMA’s emergency flood-insurance program does not affect the requirement that communities adopt adequate floodplain management regulations, which is compact evidence that emergency posture does not erase underlying land-use governance.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-44/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-59

- **[S1134] eCFR — 44 CFR 60.3 Flood plain management criteria for flood-prone areas**  
  Why it matters: requires communities to require permits for all proposed construction or other development in flood-prone areas, which reinforces that even urgent rebuilding still sits inside permit-governed land-use logic.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-44/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-60/subpart-A/section-60.3

- **[S1135] FEMA — Reimbursement for Post-Disaster Floodplain Management and Building Code Administration and Enforcement**  
  Why it matters: says that after a disaster hundreds of structures may need repairs, permits, and inspections and that FEMA may reimburse administration of floodplain-management and code duties, which is a useful present-day signal that disaster tempo increases governance load rather than suspending it.  
  URL: https://www.fema.gov/fact-sheet/fema-reimbursement-post-disaster-floodplain-management-and-building-code-0

## Stale-clearance fault classes / reason-coded stale state

- **[S1136] eCFR — 40 CFR 312.20 All appropriate inquiries**  
  Why it matters: requires all appropriate inquiries within one year before acquisition, requires several components to be conducted or updated within 180 days, and requires previously collected information to be updated for relevant changes in property conditions, which is strong official evidence that “stale” already splits into calendar-age failure and condition-change failure.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-J/part-312/subpart-C/section-312.20

- **[S1137] U.S. Army Corps of Engineers Headquarters — 12 March 2025 WOTUS announcement, AJD treatment**  
  Why it matters: states that approved jurisdictional determinations are generally valid for five years unless new information warrants revision before expiration, which is unusually direct evidence that one official determination product already distinguishes clock expiry from pre-expiry information change.  
  URL: https://www.usace.army.mil/media/announcements/article/4117958/12-march-2025-epa-and-army-announce-guidance-and-a-wotus-notice-the-final-respo/

- **[S1138] U.S. Army Corps of Engineers, Philadelphia District — Jurisdictional Determinations**  
  Why it matters: says an approved JD is an official Corps determination and is valid for five years, which helps anchor the baseline validity-clock side of the stale-fault split in a current public-facing Corps page.  
  URL: https://www.nap.usace.army.mil/Missions/Regulatory/Jurisdictional-Determinations/

- **[S1139] FEMA Flood Map Service Center — Products and Tools Overview**  
  Why it matters: says FEMA issues LOMC Revalidation or LOMC-VALID letters when previously issued LOMCs are found to still be valid during a new flood mapping study, which is strong evidence that source supersession does not produce one generic stale state but at least a split between surviving and non-surviving prior determinations.  
  URL: https://msc.fema.gov/portal/resources/productsandtools

- **[S1140] Pennsylvania One Call — Underground Utility Line Protection Law (Act 287, as amended)**  
  Why it matters: fixes the lawful start date at three to ten business days after notification, defines emergency, requires emergency responses as soon as practicable, allows limited proceed rights after failed direct contact on unmarked or incorrectly marked facilities, and prohibits misrepresentation of an emergency excavation, which together show that stale-state classes already spill into timing, fallback, and abuse-routing rules.  
  URL: https://www.pa1call.org/pocs/7bf4a38e-2dbf-43ce-89b2-aa1f03b2352e/PA-Act-287-as-amended

- **[S1141] Pennsylvania 811 — Excavators**  
  Why it matters: says a ticket does not need updating every ten business days on an active work site when markings have been preserved and equipment has not been moved off site for more than two business days, but that updates are needed when markings are compromised or work did not begin within the original lawful start date, which is a direct public taxonomy of stale-clearance subtypes in field operations.  
  URL: https://www.pa1call.org/pocs/350849e1-ae0e-4361-b513-21b28fe3b9a0/Excavators

- **[S1142] PHMSA — Damage Prevention**  
  Why it matters: says excavation damage continues to be a leading cause of pipeline incidents and can cause fatalities, injuries, environmental damages, and major costs, which explains why named stale-state fault classes may become economically and legally important rather than merely analytical.  
  URL: https://primis.phmsa.dot.gov/comm/damageprevention.htm

## Replay-quality underwriting / money-facing reconstructability

- **[S1143] Treasury — Federal Insurance Office proposed underwriting-data collection (2022)**  
  Why it matters: says Treasury sought current and historical homeowners underwriting data aggregated at ZIP Code level and described the need for consistent, granular, and comparable insurance data to assess disruptions of private coverage, which is a strong official signal that underwriting inputs can become standardized policy objects.  
  URL: https://home.treasury.gov/news/press-releases/jy1030

- **[S1144] Treasury — Federal Insurance Office report-release statement on climate-risk supervision (2023)**  
  Why it matters: says climate-related risks pose increasingly significant challenges for the insurance industry and that the proposed underwriting-data collection would provide the first quantitative assessment of its kind, which reinforces that underwriting variables are becoming analytically and supervisory legible.  
  URL: https://home.treasury.gov/news/press-releases/jy1579

- **[S1145] Treasury — FIO Reports & Notices page**  
  Why it matters: shows that revised climate-related underwriting-data collection materials and later homeowners-insurance market analyses remained active enough to become part of Treasury’s standing reports-and-notices inventory, which supports the claim that underwriting-data infrastructure is becoming durable rather than one-off.  
  URL: https://home.treasury.gov/policy-issues/financial-markets-financial-institutions-and-fiscal-service/federal-insurance-office/reports-notices

- **[S1146] FEMA — National Flood Insurance Program underwriting forms**  
  Why it matters: says the Standard Flood Hazard Determination Form is required for all federally backed loans and used by lenders to determine flood risk, which is a direct current example of a money-facing decision depending on a standardized hazard form.  
  URL: https://www.fema.gov/flood-insurance/find-form/underwriting

- **[S1147] eCFR — 12 CFR Part 22, flood hazard determination form**  
  Why it matters: requires banks and federal savings associations to use FEMA’s standard flood hazard determination form and retain the completed form for the period of time they own the loan, which is a compact official example of durable underwriting-file replayability.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-12/chapter-I/part-22

- **[S1148] Pennsylvania One Call — User Guide Text**  
  Why it matters: says POCS records conversations relating to locate requests, maintains voice and data records for five years, and assigns serial numbers to notifications, which shows that conditioned-place work already generates durable, serial-numbered evidence trails.  
  URL: https://www.pa1call.org/pocs/68a17ede-7779-4ad3-afae-653ace1e1e8a/User-Guide-Text

- **[S1149] Pennsylvania One Call — Underground Utility Line Protection Law**  
  Why it matters: requires assignment of serial numbers, legal excavation dates, and digitally logged voice transactions retained for five years, which strengthens the claim that these trails are not optional back-office notes but formal governance artifacts.  
  URL: https://www.pa1call.org/pocs/7bf4a38e-2dbf-43ce-89b2-aa1f03b2352e/PA-Act-287-as-amended

- **[S1150] EPA — Construction General Permit frequent questions**  
  Why it matters: says SWPPPs, inspection reports, and corrective-action logs may be prepared and kept electronically only if they are readable like paper records, legally dependable, and accessible during inspection, which is a direct official statement that compliance-record quality matters, not just record existence.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/npdes/construction-general-permit-cgp-frequent-questions

- **[S1151] eCFR — 40 CFR 122.41, NPDES record retention and record contents**  
  Why it matters: requires retention of monitoring information and underlying permit-application data and specifies dates, places, personnel, methods, and results that records must preserve, which shows public rules already care about reconstructability detail.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-122/subpart-C/section-122.41

- **[S1152] eCFR — 40 CFR Part 127, NPDES electronic reporting**  
  Why it matters: requires timely, accurate, complete, and nationally consistent electronic data transfers, which is current official evidence that record quality can already be operationalized and audited rather than merely narrated.  
  URL: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-40/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-127

- **[S1153] PHMSA — Damage Prevention**  
  Why it matters: says excavation damage remains a leading cause of pipeline incidents, highlights fatalities, injuries, environmental damage, property damage, and costs, and points to state-specific data plus a heat map, which explains why replay quality can plausibly become financially salient.  
  URL: https://primis.phmsa.dot.gov/comm/damageprevention.htm

  Why it matters: says users can review all changes made to NFIP maps in a given six-month period and submit formal map-change requests, which is a compact public signal that official hazard sources are actively revised and that downstream users may need structured change-tracking rather than one-time lookup.

- **[S1157] PA PUC — Damage Prevention Committee**  
  Why it matters: says alleged violations of the One Call Law are reported through the One Call system to the Pennsylvania Public Utility Commission, which is direct evidence that excavation-response and override disputes already have a formal intake path rather than remaining purely private arguments.

- **[S1158] PA PUC — Damage Prevention Committee Announces Actions (Jan. 5, 2026)**  
  Why it matters: says the DPC meets regularly to review alleged violations and can issue warning letters, mandatory training, and administrative penalties, which is strong evidence that One Call response and excavation disputes are already being processed as a standing review class.

- **[S1159] PA PUC — National 8-1-1 Day enforcement update (Aug. 11, 2025)**  
  Why it matters: says Pennsylvania’s 2024 law changes expanded enforcement authority, made reporting of alleged violations mandatory, accelerated timelines, and emphasized escalating accountability, which is a compact official signal that the state is deliberately thickening the post-incident review layer around dig-safe and emergency claims.

  Why it matters: shows concrete case summaries combining emergency-response failures, late alleged-violation reports, due-care failures, compliance-training requirements, and administrative penalties, which makes it unusually direct evidence that override and response disputes are already recurring review objects rather than isolated anecdotes.

- **[S1161] PA PUC — Damage Prevention Committee / statistical-report page**  
  Why it matters: says the Act 127 Statistical Report uses enforcement data from the start of PUC enforcement to examine current violation trends, which is direct evidence that line-strike and response failures are already being aggregated into trend surfaces rather than kept only as isolated cases.

- **[S1162] PA PUC — DPC enforcement actions announcement (Jan. 26, 2026)**

- **[S1163] PHMSA — National Pipeline Performance Measures**  
  Why it matters: says excavation damage remains a leading cause of life-threatening incidents, tracks incidents/leaks/damages caused by excavation damage, includes one-call-ticket counts in gas-distribution integrity-management performance, and says PHMSA uses performance measures to monitor progress and prioritize operators for inspection.  
  URL: https://www.phmsa.dot.gov/data-and-statistics/pipeline/national-pipeline-performance-measures

- **[S1164] PHMSA — State Oversight**

- **[S1165] EPA ECHO — Tool Guide**  
  Why it matters: says ECHO provides EPA/State Comparative Maps and State Dashboards with charts and summary information about compliance and enforcement metrics at national or state levels, which is a clear official example of scoreboard-style governance surfaces.

- **[S1166] EPA — Enforcement Data and Results**  
  Why it matters: says EPA uses enforcement and compliance data for managing the program and assessing performance, and highlights ECHO plus the State Review Framework, which supports the claim that comparative compliance data routinely shapes oversight rather than only retrospective reporting.

- **[S1167] FEMA — NFIP pricing approach / CRS classification**  
  Why it matters: says communities can earn flood-insurance premium discounts of 5% to 45% based on Community Rating System classification, which is a compact official example of a place-governance rating surface changing price.

- **[S1168] FEMA — OpenFEMA NFIP Community Status Book dataset**  
  Why it matters: says the Community Status Book dataset contains the current NFIP status of a community and that this status determines whether citizens have the opportunity to purchase flood insurance, which shows that eligibility itself can hinge on a maintained public status surface.

## Scoreboard-appeal workflows / governance service tier

- **[S1169] EPA ECHO — How to Report an Error**  
  Why it matters: says ECHO error reports enter EPA’s Integrated Error Correction Process, are routed to the appropriate data steward, generate acknowledgement, and may trigger requests for added documentation, which is direct evidence that consequential public compliance surfaces already rely on standing correction workflows.  
  URL: https://echo.epa.gov/help/how-to-report-error

- **[S1170] EPA ECHO — Frequently Asked Questions**  
  Why it matters: says facilities and members of the public can submit corrections, that responsible officials review those requests, and that resolved violations can be redesignated when documentation is provided, which shows public ranking surfaces already need a governed path for correction and status revision.  
  URL: https://echo.epa.gov/resources/general-info/echo-faq

- **[S1171] EPA ECHO — Detailed Facility Report Data Dictionary**  
  Why it matters: says recent compliance-quarter data are draft, subject to change, and paired with report-error tools before they become official, which is unusually direct evidence that comparative public status surfaces already assume a draft-to-official correction cycle.  
  URL: https://echo.epa.gov/help/reports/dfr-data-dictionary

- **[S1172] USACE North Atlantic Division — Appeals Process**  
  Why it matters: says applicants and landowners may appeal denied permits and jurisdictional determinations within 60 days, that site visits or appeal conferences may occur, and that decisions can be remanded when the record lacked accurate or sufficient information, which is a strong official model of redress for consequential classificatory decisions.  
  URL: https://www.nad.usace.army.mil/Business-With-Us/Regulatory-Permitting/Appeals-Process/

- **[S1173] NRCS — Conservation Compliance Appeals Process**  
  Why it matters: says preliminary technical determinations become final after 30 days if not appealed, that reconsideration can trigger an additional field visit, and that further appeals can go to FSA or the National Appeals Division, which shows a current multi-step correction-and-hearing grammar for land-affecting determinations.  
  URL: https://www.nrcs.usda.gov/getting-assistance/financial-help/conservation-compliance-appeals-process

- **[S1174] FEMA — Flood Hazard Determination Notices and Appeal Periods**  
  Why it matters: says communities receive a 90-day appeal period for new or modified flood hazard determinations, that adversely affected owners or lessees may appeal based on scientific or technical evidence, and that other errors are handled through a comment path, which is direct evidence that official map-based status surfaces already separate formal appeals from lighter correction channels.  
  URL: https://floodmaps.fema.gov/fhm/bfe_status/bfe_main.asp

- **[S1175] FEMA Mapping Information Platform — Directory definitions**  
  Why it matters: defines a Letter of Determination Review as FEMA’s response to a borrower-and-lender request asking whether FEMA concurs with the lender’s flood determination for a building, which shows an official property-specific redress path beneath broader map appeals.  
  URL: https://hazards.fema.gov/femaportal/wps/portal/%21ut/p/z1/04_Sj9CPykssy0xPLMnMz0vMAfIjo8zifQI83D38vQ38LRxNXAwCnZ1DvEL9XA0N3M30wwkpiAJKG-AAjgZA_VFgJXATDPwtXQ0CTYwNA4JNHY0MAo2hCvCYUZAbYZDpqKgIAJyVC1g%21/dz/d5/L2dJQSEvUUt3QS80TmxFL1o2X0xQSEdIT0swTzBGUzkwUUMzRDNRTTUzMDYw/

- **[S1176] PA PUC — One Call formal-complaint authority (McClain Bros. complaint)**  
  Why it matters: says Commission prosecutorial staff may bring formal complaints against entities subject to the PA One Call Law and that the Commission may hear and determine those complaints, which shows that informal damage-prevention determinations can escalate into formal evidentiary proceedings.  
  URL: https://www.puc.pa.gov/pcdocs/1837237.pdf

- **[S1177] PA PUC — Formal Complaints**  
  Why it matters: says formal complaints are legal proceedings before an administrative law judge, with answers, hearings, evidence, and later Commission rulings, which supports the claim that a published or informal score eventually needs a governed redress lane rather than mere customer service.  
  URL: https://www.puc.pa.gov/complaints/formal-complaints/


## Determination-state machines / draft-final-effective calendars

- **[S1178] EPA ECHO — Release Notes**  
  Why it matters: says recently reported data are considered draft so permittees can identify possible data-entry errors before an official quarterly noncompliance status is calculated, which is direct evidence that an important public compliance surface already has an explicit draft-to-official state machine.

- **[S1180] NRCS — Conservation Compliance Appeals Process**  
  Why it matters: says initial determinations are issued as Preliminary Technical Determinations and become final after 30 days if not appealed, which shows that the same land-affecting content carries different practical meaning at different states in its lifecycle.

- **[S1181] FEMA — Flood Hazard Determination Notices and Appeal Periods**

- **[S1182] FEMA — Lifecycle of a Risk MAP Project**  
  Why it matters: says that after appeals are resolved FEMA sends a Letter of Final Determination and the maps become effective six months later, which is unusually direct evidence that finality and effectiveness are separate operational states.

- **[S1183] USACE North Atlantic Division — Appeals Process**  
  Why it matters: says certain jurisdictional determinations and permit decisions can be appealed within 60 days and may be remanded after review, which shows that even after issuance a consequential decision can remain in an appealable, non-settled state.

  Why it matters: says parties can request to be notified when the district posts a new public notice and can join distribution lists, which shows that permit- and decision-adjacent state changes already rely on formal notification routing even before any broader cross-system synchronization exists.

## Provisional-use policies / interim admissibility

- **[S1187] US EPA — Administrative Continuance of EPA’s 2021 MSGP**  
  Why it matters: says facilities already covered before permit expiration automatically remain covered and must keep complying, while new facilities cannot obtain coverage under the continued permit and may only operate under a bounded No Action Assurance that does not itself confer permit coverage, which is unusually direct evidence of a formal provisional-use regime.  
  URL: https://www.epa.gov/npdes/administrative-continuance-epas-2021-msgp

- **[S1188] USACE Philadelphia District — Jurisdictional Determinations**  
  Why it matters: says preliminary JDs are advisory, may not be appealed, and may be elected by an affected party who wants to move ahead expeditiously to obtain a Corps permit authorization, which is direct evidence that a provisional determination can support bounded action without being fully dispositive.  
  URL: https://www.nap.usace.army.mil/Missions/Regulatory/Jurisdictional-Determinations/

- **[S1189] FEMA Mapping Information Platform — Preliminary Flood Hazard Data**  
  Why it matters: says preliminary flood hazard data are for review and guidance only, are subject to change, are not final, and cannot be used to rate flood insurance or enforce the federal mandatory purchase requirement, which is explicit official evidence that interim status carries a sharply bounded use policy.  
  URL: https://hazards.fema.gov/femaportal/prelimdownload/

- **[S1190] FEMA Flood Map Service Center — Products and Tools Overview**  
  Why it matters: says preliminary products provide an early look at projected risk during an in-progress study and are not final, which reinforces that FEMA already distinguishes visibility from full regulatory usability in a way other sectors may increasingly need to formalize.  
  URL: https://msc.fema.gov/portal/resources/productsandtools

- **[S1191] FEMA Flood Map Service Center — Search All Products**

- **[S1192] FEMA Flood Map Service Center — Welcome**

- **[S1193] FEMA — Flood Insurance Rules and Legislation**  
  Why it matters: FEMA’s current rules-and-legislation material explicitly treats grandfathering and map updates as live program topics, and the page summary notes that the 2014 changes restored grandfathered rates, which is unusually direct evidence that preservation of older treatment has money implications rather than merely archival ones.

- **[S1194] FEMA — Real Estate, Lending and Insurance Professionals**

- **[S1195] Delta County, Colorado — Frequently Asked Questions (Land Use Code / legal nonconforming uses)**

- **[S1196] City of Rochester — Zoning Applications / Certificate of Nonconformity**

- **[S1199] FDA — Enforcement Reports**

- **[S1200] FDA — Recalls, Outbreaks & Emergencies**

## Notice authenticity / anti-spoofing / trusted routing

- **[S1202] FEMA — IPAWS All-Hazards Information Feed**  
  Why it matters: says alerts are digitally signed by the alerting authority to ensure authenticity and prevent spoofing, which is unusually direct official evidence that routed notice itself now requires proof-bearing trust controls rather than mere publication.  
  URL: https://www.fema.gov/emergency-managers/practitioners/integrated-public-alert-warning-system/technology-developers/all-hazards-information-feed

- **[S1203] OASIS Open — CAP 1.2 Receives Approval as OASIS Standard**  
  Why it matters: says CAP 1.2 added digital signature support offering added security and authentication for next-generation alerting systems, and frames CAP as a structured multi-network alert format supported by both public and private actors, which is strong evidence that authenticity has already become a standards-layer requirement for serious notice systems.  
  URL: https://www.oasis-open.org/2010/08/11/common-alerting-protocol-cap-1-2-receives-approval-as-oasis-standard/

- **[S1204] CISA — Insights on Enhance Email & Web Security**  
  Why it matters: says SPF, DKIM, and DMARC authenticate mail senders and that DMARC set to reject provides the strongest protection against spoofed email by ensuring unauthenticated messages are rejected, which is direct evidence that high-value notice channels increasingly rely on explicit anti-spoof controls.  
  URL: https://www.cisa.gov/sites/default/files/publications/CISAInsights-Cyber-EnhanceEmailandWebSecurity_S508C-a.pdf

- **[S1205] CISA — Cybersecurity Performance Goals**  
  Why it matters: says DMARC should be enabled and set to reject, which shows that strong anti-spoof configuration is now treated as baseline operational practice rather than optional hardening.  
  URL: https://www.cisa.gov/cybersecurity-performance-goals-cpgs

- **[S1206] FCC — Call Authentication**  
  Why it matters: says caller-ID authentication technology enables subscribers to trust that callers are who they say they are and reduces the effectiveness of fraudulently spoofed calls, which extends the notice-authenticity problem beyond email and alert feeds into voice workflows that also trigger action.  
  URL: https://www.fcc.gov/call-authentication


## Delivery proof / receipt evidence / routed-notice results


- **[S1207] USPS — Shipping Insurance & Delivery Services**  
  Why it matters: says Certified Mail can show when an item was delivered or a delivery attempt was made, that Return Receipt provides an electronic or physical delivery record showing the recipient’s signature, and that USPS Tracking Plus can preserve extended tracking history for up to 10 years, which is direct evidence that delivery proof is already a tiered service rather than a generic byproduct.  
  URL: https://www.usps.com/ship/insurance-extra-services.htm

- **[S1208] PACER — When a user files a pleading with the court, does the system automatically serve the other parties?**  
  Why it matters: says a Notice of Electronic Filing or Notice of Docket Activity is automatically generated and emailed to registered case parties, and that the notice includes the docket text, unique electronic document stamp, list of participants receiving notification, and document link, which is unusually direct evidence that formal electronic service already treats recipient-scope delivery records as part of the notice artifact itself.

- **[S1209] AWS — Amazon SNS message delivery status**  
  Why it matters: says Amazon SNS supports delivery-status logging to CloudWatch Logs and that those logs let operators determine whether a message was successfully delivered, identify the endpoint response, and measure dwell time, which is direct evidence that route-level delivery evidence has already become ordinary operations infrastructure in machine-routed systems.  
  URL: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sns/latest/dg/sns-topic-attributes.html

- **[S1210] Twilio — Track the Message Status of Outbound Messages**  
  Why it matters: says Twilio status callbacks track outbound-message lifecycle changes through delivery and even read receipt on supporting channels, and that callback payloads can include failure detail and carrier delivery-receipt timing, which shows that mainstream communications vendors already expose delivery-result evidence as a maintained programmable surface.  
  URL: https://www.twilio.com/docs/messaging/guides/track-outbound-message-status


## Nonreceipt reason codes / failure grammars / remedy routing

- **[S1211] IETF — RFC 3463: Enhanced Mail System Status Codes**  
  Why it matters: says the standard defines extended status codes for delivery status reports, tracking, and improved diagnostics, and structures them so a message can carry machine-readable success / transient failure / persistent failure plus the probable source and precise condition of the anomaly, which is direct evidence that nonreceipt is already treated as a reason-coded reporting problem rather than a binary outcome.  
  URL: https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc3463

- **[S1212] USPS Postal Explorer — DMM 507 Mailer Services**  
  Why it matters: says undeliverable-as-addressed mail is endorsed with the reason for nondelivery and lists distinct official outcomes such as attempted-not-known, insufficient address, moved-left-no-address, refused, unclaimed, and unable to forward, which is direct evidence that physical notice infrastructure already runs on an operational nondelivery taxonomy.  
  URL: https://pe.usps.com/text/dmm300/507.htm

- **[S1213] USPS PostalPro — Undeliverable-as-Addressed (UAA) Mail Statistics**  
  Why it matters: summarizes current USPS UAA causes as moved addressee, incomplete / incorrect / illegible address, unknown or deceased addressee, refusal or failure to claim, and missing postage, which is useful official shorthand for the broader reason-coded failure pattern.  
  URL: https://postalpro.usps.com/address-quality-solutions/undeliverable-addressed-uaa-mail

- **[S1214] AWS — Amazon SNS notification contents for Amazon SES**  
  Why it matters: documents bounce types and subtypes such as permanent, transient, suppressed recipient, unsubscribed recipient, mailbox full, content rejected, and attachment rejected, and also carries diagnostic code information when available, which is direct evidence that software-mediated notice systems already emit machine-readable nonreceipt reasons that imply different remedy paths.  
  URL: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/ses/latest/dg/notification-contents.html

- **[S1215] Twilio — Messages resource**  
  Why it matters: documents message lifecycle states including failed, delivered, and undelivered, and says the API returns an error code when a message is failed or undelivered, which shows that mainstream communications layers already expose reason-bearing failure outputs rather than a simple sent / unsent bit.  
  URL: https://www.twilio.com/docs/messaging/api/message-resource

- **[S1216] FINRA — Business Continuity Planning FAQ**

- **[S1217] CMS — Core EP Rule Elements**  
  Why it matters: says providers need a communication plan with a system to contact staff, physicians, and other necessary persons, and that emergency-preparedness plans and training/testing material must be reviewed and updated at least annually, which is direct evidence that healthcare escalation paths are governed as maintained contact systems.

  Why it matters: says organizations should conduct plan testing, training, and exercises periodically and gives testing call-tree lists within prescribed time limits as an explicit example, which is unusually direct evidence that contact-route operability is something institutions already know how to test rather than merely assume.

- **[S1219] AWS — Types of Amazon Route 53 health checks**  
  Why it matters: says Route 53 can submit automated requests at regular intervals to verify that an endpoint is reachable, available, and functional, which is direct evidence that endpoint liveness checking is already ordinary infrastructure for machine-routed escalation paths.

- **[S1220] Atlassian Opsgenie — Add Heartbeats to monitor systems**  
  Why it matters: says Heartbeats are used to ensure continuous connectivity and expected task execution, and that if a heartbeat is not received within the configured interval Opsgenie creates an alert, which shows that mainstream operations tooling already sells liveness checking as a maintained service layer rather than an ad hoc manual check.  
  URL: https://support.atlassian.com/opsgenie/docs/add-heartbeats-to-monitor-external-systems/



- **[S1221] FINRA Rule 4370 — Business Continuity Plans and Emergency Contact Information**  
  Why it matters: requires member firms to designate emergency contact persons, promptly update emergency contact information upon material change, and review and update those designations, which is direct evidence that named responsibility holders are treated as maintained operational bindings rather than static contacts.  
  URL: https://www.finra.org/rules-guidance/rulebooks/finra-rules/4370

- **[S1222] Microsoft Entra — What are access reviews?**  
  Why it matters: says access reviews can recur weekly, monthly, quarterly, or annually, which is direct evidence that institutions already operationalize periodic freshness checks on who should still hold roles or access.  
  URL: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/id-governance/access-reviews-overview

- **[S1223] Microsoft Learn — Microsoft cloud security benchmark v2: Privileged Access**  
  Why it matters: says reviewers should evaluate whether assigned roles still align with current responsibilities, document reasons for retaining access, and remove stale or misconfigured accounts, which is unusually direct evidence that role-to-responsibility freshness is already governed as an ongoing review object.  
  URL: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/security/benchmark/azure/mcsb-v2-privileged-access

- **[S1224] AWS — Security best practices in IAM**  
  Why it matters: says organizations should regularly review and remove unused users, roles, permissions, policies, and credentials, using last-accessed information to identify what no longer needs to exist, which is direct evidence that stale responsibility bindings are already treated as an operational risk.  
  URL: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/best-practices.html

- **[S1225] PagerDuty — Escalation Policies and Schedules**  
  Why it matters: says escalation policies connect services to on-call schedules to ensure the right people are notified at the right time, which is direct evidence that modern incident tooling already sells live responsibility as a maintained binding between service, schedule, and named owner.  
  URL: https://support.pagerduty.com/main/docs/escalation-policies-and-schedules

- **[S1226] PagerDuty — Contact Information**  
  Why it matters: recommends secondary contact methods as backups, requires verification for SMS methods before they can receive notifications, and supports testing contact methods, which is direct evidence that backup delegates and notification paths already carry explicit freshness and verification burdens.

## Delegate-change propagation / eventual consistency / change-correlation

- **[S1227] Google Cloud — Access change propagation**  
  Why it matters: says IAM access changes are eventually consistent, policy edits typically propagate in about 2 minutes but can take longer, group-membership changes can take several minutes or hours, adding to a group usually propagates faster than removing, and nested-group changes propagate more slowly still, which is unusually direct evidence that delegate-change diffusion has real lag classes and asymmetries.  
  URL: https://cloud.google.com/iam/docs/access-change-propagation

- **[S1228] AWS — Troubleshoot IAM**  
  Why it matters: says changes to users, groups, roles, policies, and ABAC tags take time to become visible from all possible endpoints because of distributed replication and caching, and recommends not placing such IAM changes in critical high-availability code paths, which is strong evidence that authoritative-change time and effective-change time are already treated as different operational moments.  
  URL: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/troubleshoot.html

- **[S1229] Microsoft Learn — Change resource roles for an access package in entitlement management**  
  Why it matters: says assignment and resource-role changes can take up to 24 hours to be made in Microsoft Entra ID, plus additional time to propagate to Microsoft Online Services or connected SaaS applications, and points administrators to audit and provisioning logs to monitor progress, which is direct evidence that downstream propagation delay is already a managed operational concern.  
  URL: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/id-governance/entitlement-management-access-package-resources

- **[S1230] Microsoft Learn — Continuous access evaluation in Microsoft Entra**  
  Why it matters: says Conditional Access policy and group-membership changes can take up to one day to become effective because of replication between Microsoft Entra and resource providers such as Exchange Online and SharePoint Online, which is unusually direct evidence that correct central state can coexist with temporarily stale dependent enforcement surfaces.  
  URL: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/identity/conditional-access/concept-continuous-access-evaluation

- **[S1231] PagerDuty — Recent Changes**  
  Why it matters: says incident pages show recent change correlations from the past 24 hours and can surface context like “This incident occurred X minutes/hours after this change,” which is strong evidence that mainstream incident tooling already treats recent change diffusion as first-class context for explaining failures.  
  URL: https://support.pagerduty.com/main/docs/recent-changes

- **[S1232] AWS — Identity-based policies for Amazon EC2**

- **[S1237] Okta — Edit app provisioning settings**  
  Why it matters: lets administrators schedule incremental and full imports hourly, daily, or weekly, which shows that identity-convergence timing is already treated as an explicit operator choice with downstream reliability consequences.

- **[S1238] GitHub Docs — Troubleshooting identity and access management for your enterprise**  
  Why it matters: says the default SCIM provisioning interval for Microsoft Entra ID is 40 minutes, which shows that downstream SaaS operators already normalize a specific external sync interval as a practical operational fact customers must account for.

## Convergence-proof gates / verification checkpoints / deployment protection

- **[S1239] Google Cloud — Test changes to allow policies**  
  Why it matters: says risky IAM allow-policy changes can disrupt active users or service accounts and recommends testing changes with Policy Simulator before committing them, which is direct evidence that some state changes already acquire a pre-commit proof checkpoint rather than relying on blind application.  
  URL: https://docs.cloud.google.com/iam/docs/test-policy-changes

- **[S1240] AWS — Troubleshoot IAM**  
  Why it matters: says operators should verify IAM changes have propagated before production workflows depend on them, which is unusually direct evidence that elapsed time alone is treated as insufficient for higher-consequence proceed decisions.

- **[S1241] Microsoft Entra ID — On-demand provisioning using cloud sync**  
  Why it matters: says on-demand provisioning helps administrators validate and verify that configuration changes were applied properly and are being correctly synchronized, which shows convergence proof already being offered as an explicit workflow step.

- **[S1242] Kubernetes — Deployments**  
  Why it matters: says a Deployment is complete only when all replicas are updated and available and no old replicas remain, and that `kubectl rollout status` returns success only when rollout completion is reached, which is direct evidence of a standardized proceed/hold checkpoint around convergence.  
  URL: https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/workloads/controllers/deployment/

- **[S1243] Google Cloud Deploy — Verify your deployment**  
  Why it matters: says Cloud Deploy can run verification tasks after deployment finishes and that if verification fails the rollout fails, which shows post-deploy proof gates already determining whether a release may count as successfully advanced.

- **[S1244] Google Cloud Deploy — Run analysis jobs in Cloud Deploy**  
  Why it matters: says analysis jobs run after deploy and verify, can fail a rollout on bad telemetry, and can support automatic rollback or controlled promotion, which shows ongoing convergence proof being used as an explicit release gate rather than as optional observation.  
  URL: https://docs.cloud.google.com/deploy/docs/analysis

- **[S1245] GitHub Docs — Deployments and environments**  
  Why it matters: says deployment protection rules require conditions to pass before a job referencing an environment can proceed and can use manual approval, delays, or third-party readiness systems, which is direct evidence that workflow progression is already being formalized as gated convergence proof.

## Proceed-before-convergence waivers / emergency change paths / override trails

- **[S1256] GitHub Docs — Reviewing deployments**
